Automorphisms of 𝑷 𝟏 \boldsymbol{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\! -bundles over rational surfaces
Jérémy Blanc, Andrea Fanelli, Ronan Terpereau

Abstract




Abstract.
In this paper, we provide the complete classification of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over smooth projective rational surfaces whose neutral component of the automorphism group is maximal. Our results hold over any algebraically closed field of characteristic zero.

Keywords.
Rational threefolds, vector bundles,
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles, automorphisms, birational maps, algebraic groups, classification

1. Introduction

1.1. Aim and scope

In this article, we work over an algebraically closed field
\mathrm{k}
of characteristic zero (even if most of the partial results we obtain along the way are valid over any algebraically closed field
\mathrm{k}
, as we explain at the beginning of each section). We study
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
X\to S
(always assumed to be locally trivial for the Zariski topology), where
S
is a smooth projective rational surface (the smoothness condition is actually not a strong restriction; see §
2.2
), and study the group scheme
\Aut(X)
of automorphisms of
X
. This group scheme can have infinitely many components, but the connected component of the identity
\Autz(X)
is a connected algebraic group; see
[
MO67
]
. The aim of this article is to study the pairs
(X,\Autz(X))
and to classify these, up to birational conjugation. In particular, we describe the geometry of the pairs arising and the restriction of
X\to S
to natural curves of
S
.

Our motivation for this study comes from the classification of connected algebraic subgroups of the Cremona group
\Bir(\mathbb{P}^{3})
, stated by Enriques and Fano in
[
EF98
]
and achieved by Umemura over the field
\mathrm{k}=\mathbb{C}
in a series of four papers
[
Ume80
,
Ume82a
,
Ume82b
,
Ume85
]
, using analytic methods. As explained in
[
MU83
,
Ume88
]
, these groups act on some minimal rational threefolds, and it turns out that most of the threefolds obtained are
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over rational surfaces. Our plan is to give a shorter geometric proof of the classification of Umemura, and this article is the first step in this direction (the second step is in
[
BFT21
]
). Instead of starting with the group action and trying to find threefolds where the group acts, we will directly study the possible varieties and their symmetries, then reduce to some simple varieties, and in the end compute the neutral component of their automorphism groups.

Our approach should be seen as the analogue of the following way to understand the classification of connected algebraic subgroups of the Cremona group
\Bir(\mathbb{P}^{2})
. This classification was initiated by Enriques in
[
Enr93
]
and can nowadays be easily recovered via the classification of smooth projective rational surfaces, as we now explain. One can conjugate any connected algebraic subgroup of
\Bir(\mathbb{P}^{2})
to a group of automorphisms of a smooth projective rational surface
S
. Contracting all
(-1)
-curves of
S
, we can moreover assume that
S
is a minimal surface,
i.e.
that
S
is isomorphic to the projective plane
\mathbb{P}^{2}
or a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{a}
, with
a\geq 0
,
a\neq 1
; see
[
Bea96
]
for a general survey on surfaces. One then checks that the groups of automorphisms obtained are maximal, as these have no orbit of finite size: this forbids the existence of equivariant birational maps towards other smooth projective rational surfaces. Every connected algebraic subgroup of
\Bir(\mathbb{P}^{2})
is thus contained in a maximal connected algebraic subgroup of
\Bir(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, which is conjugate to the group
\Autz(S)
, where
S
is a minimal smooth rational surface. See also
[
Bla09
]
for the classification of the (not necessarily connected) maximal algebraic subgroups of
\Bir(\mathbb{P}^{2})
with a similar approach.

The aim of our classification is then to proceed as in the case of surfaces and study minimal threefolds. There are many more such varieties in dimension
3
than in dimension
2
, and not all of them yield maximal algebraic subgroups of
\Bir(\mathbb{P}^{3})
. Moreover, some maximal connected algebraic subgroups of
\Bir(\mathbb{P}^{3})
are realised by infinitely many minimal threefolds. Our aim is then to understand the geometry of the minimal threefolds obtained in this way and the equivariant birational maps between them.

Another motivation consists in unifying some well-known results (most of them over the field of complex numbers, using topological arguments) on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over minimal rational surfaces,
i.e.
the projective plane
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and the Hirzebruch surfaces
\mathbb{F}_{a}
, and to obtain these results from the perspective of automorphisms groups. See for instance Corollaries
3.3.3
and
4.2.2
and Remark
4.3.2
.

We also provide moduli spaces parametrising the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
over Hirzebruch surfaces having no jumping fibre (see Corollary
3.3.8
),
i.e.
the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles such that all fibres of the natural morphism
X\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
(induced by the structure morphism
\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
) are Hirzebruch surfaces
\mathbb{F}_{b}
for a fixed
b
not depending on the fibre. These
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles can also be described using exact sequences of vector bundles (see Corollary
3.3.3
). The action of
\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{a})
on the moduli spaces, depending on some natural numerical invariants (see Definition
1.4.1
), is described explicitly; see §
3.4
, and more precisely Corollary
3.4.6
.

Our approach uses very basic tools and is aimed to be easy to follow by any interested reader, not necessarily expert. Most of the time we give proofs which do not use any known results, and we make a reference to the literature when we re-prove a known fact.

1.2. Summary of the classification

Definition 1.2.1
.

Let
\pi\colon X\to S
and
\pi^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
be two
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over two smooth projective rational surfaces
S
and
S^{\prime}
. A birational map
\varphi\colon X\dashrightarrow X^{\prime}
is said to be

(1)

a
square birational map
(
resp. square isomorphism/square automorphism
)
if there exists a birational map
\eta\colon S\dashrightarrow S^{\prime}
such that
\pi^{\prime}\varphi=\eta\pi
(and if
\varphi
is resp. an isomorphism/automorphism)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\hbox to0.0pt{;\hss}}

we say in these cases that
\varphi
is
above

\eta
;

(2)

a
birational map
(
resp. isomorphism/automorphism
)
of
\,\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles
if
S=S^{\prime}
and
\pi^{\prime}\varphi=\pi
(and if
\varphi
is resp. an isomorphism/automorphism)

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{X^{\prime}\hbox to0.0pt{;\hss}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S}

(3)

\Autz(X)
-equivariant
if
\varphi\Autz(X)\varphi^{-1}\subset\Autz(X^{\prime})
(or, equivalently, if
\varphi\Autz(X)\varphi^{-1}\subset\Aut(X^{\prime})
).

As the definition depends on
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
, and not only on
X
and
X^{\prime}
, we will often write
\varphi\colon(X,\pi)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
and say that
(X,\pi)
and
(X^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
are, respectively, square birational/square isomorphic/birational
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles/isomorphic
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles if
\varphi
satisfies the corresponding condition.

Remark 1.2.2
.

In the above definition, every element of
\Autz(X)
is a square automorphism (see Lemma
2.1.1
) but not necessarily a birational map of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles.

Definition 1.2.3
.

Let
\pi\colon X\to S
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over a smooth projective rational surface
S
. We say that
\Autz(X)
is
maximal
if for each
\Autz(X)
-equivariant square birational map
\varphi\colon(X,\pi)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
, we have
\varphi\Autz(X)\varphi^{-1}=\Autz(X^{\prime})
. If we moreover have
(X^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})\simeq(X,\pi)
(resp.
\varphi
is an isomorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles) for each such
\varphi
, we say that the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
(X,\pi)
is
stiff
(resp.
superstiff
).

Remark 1.2.4
.

This definition depends on
X
and
\pi
, and not only on
X
. For instance, taking
X=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{F}_{1}
and two standard
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle structures
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\pi^{\prime}\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{1}
,
\Autz(X)
is maximal with respect to
\pi
but not with respect to
\pi^{\prime}
. (The pairs
(X,\pi)
and
(X,\pi^{\prime})
correspond, respectively, to
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{1,0}
and
\mathcal{F}_{1}^{0,0}
, see Definition
3.1.1
, so this observation follows from Theorem
A
).

Remark 1.2.5
.

To the best of our knowledge, the notion of stiff/superstiff is new. It is analogous to the notion of equivariant birational rigidity/superrigidity for Mori fibre spaces but is not equivalent since here we only consider
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles. Moreover, birational rigidity for Mori fibre spaces is always up to squares, while stiffness also detects these birational maps; see
[
Cor00
]
and
[
Puk13
]
to know more about the notions of rigidity and superrigidity for Mori fibre spaces.

The next statement, which summarises most of our work, is our main result (the notation is explained after Theorem
B
).

Theorem A
.

Let
\pi\colon X\to S
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over a smooth projective rational surface
S
. Then, there exists an
\Autz(X)
-equivariant square birational map
(X,\pi)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
such that
\Autz(X^{\prime})
is maximal. Moreover, the group
\Autz(X)
is maximal if and only if
(X,\pi)
is square isomorphic to one of the following:

(a)

a decomposable

\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle

\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}

\longrightarrow

\mathbb{F}_{a}

with
a,b\geq 0
,
a\neq 1
,
c\in\mathbb{Z}
,

c\leq 0
if
b=0
,

and where
a=0
or
b=c=0

or
-a<c<ab
;

(b)

a decomposable

\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle

\mathcal{P}_{b}

\longrightarrow

\mathbb{P}^{2}

for some
b\geq 0
;

(c)

an Umemura

\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle

\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}

\longrightarrow

\mathbb{F}_{a}

for some
a,b\geq 1,c\geq 2
,

with
c-ab<2
if
a\geq 2
,

and
c-ab<1
if
a=1
;

(d)

a Schwarzenberger

\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle

\mathcal{S}_{b}

\longrightarrow

\mathbb{P}^{2}

for some
b\geq 1
; or

(e)

a

\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle

\mathcal{V}_{b}

\longrightarrow

\mathbb{P}^{2}

for some
b\geq 2
.

Remark 1.2.6
.

We keep the notation of Theorem
A
:

\bullet

\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
is described in Lemma
3.1.5
and Remark
3.1.6
;

\bullet

\Autz(\mathcal{P}_{b})
is described in Lemma
4.1.2
and Remark
4.1.3
;

\bullet

\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})
is described in Lemma
3.6.3
and Remark
3.6.4
;

\bullet

\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})
is described in Lemma
4.2.5
(2)
; and

\bullet

\Autz(\mathcal{V}_{b})
is described in Lemma
5.5.1
(3)
–
(4)
.

Contrary to the dimension
2
case, there are many
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
X\to S
with maximal
\Autz(X)
which are birationally conjugate.
This means that the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles of Theorem
A
are not always stiff. The next result describes all the possible links between such
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles. The construction of such links is detailed in §
5
.

Theorem B
.

The
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles of Theorem
A
are superstiff only in the cases:

(
a
)

\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
with
a=0
or
b=c=0
;

(
b
)

\mathcal{P}_{b}
for
b\geq 0
; and

(
c
)

\mathcal{S}_{1}\simeq\mathbb{P}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
.

In the other cases, the equivariant square birational maps between the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles of Theorem
A
are given by compositions of square isomorphisms of
\,\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles and of birational maps appearing in the following list:

(1)

for all integers
a,b\geq 0
,
c\in\mathbb{Z}
with
a\neq 1
,
-a<c<0
, an infinite sequence of equivariant birational maps of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles

\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a}\dashrightarrow\cdots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+n,c+an}\dashrightarrow\cdots;

(2)

for all integers
a,b\geq 1
with
(a,b)\neq(1,1)
, an infinite sequence of equivariant birational maps of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles

\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,2}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+1,2+a}\dashrightarrow\cdots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+n,2+an}{\dashrightarrow}\cdots;

(3)

for each
b\geq 2
, a birational involution
\mathcal{S}_{b}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{b}
;

(4)

for each
b\geq 2
, the equivariant birational morphisms
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b,2}\to\mathcal{V}_{b}
obtained by contracting the preimage of the
(-1)
-curve of
\,\mathbb{F}_{1}
onto the fibre of a point of
\,\mathbb{P}^{2}
in
\mathcal{V}_{b}
.

In Theorems
A
and
B
, decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles are simply the projectivisations of decomposable rank
2
vector bundles. The
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{F}_{a}
and
\mathbb{P}^{2}
are particularly easy to describe (see §§
3.1
and
4.1
). The Schwarzenberger
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
are projectivisations of the classical rank
2
vector bundles of the same name (see below). We describe the notation here.

Definition 1.2.7

(Schwarzenberger
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles)
.

Let
b\geq-1
be an integer, and let
\kappa\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the
(2:1)
-cover defined by

\begin{array}[]{rccc}\kappa\colon&\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}&\to&\mathbb{P}^{2}\\
&\big([y_{0}:y_{1}],[z_{0}:z_{1}]\big)&\mapsto&[y_{0}z_{0}:y_{0}z_{1}+y_{1}z_{0}:y_{1}z_{1}],\end{array}

whose branch locus is the diagonal
\Delta\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and whose ramification locus is the smooth conic
\Gamma=\{[X:Y:Z]\mid Y^{2}=4XZ\}\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}.

The
b
-th Schwarzenberger
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle

\mathcal{S}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle defined by

\mathcal{S}_{b}=\mathbb{P}\big(\kappa_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-b-1,0)\big)\to\mathbb{P}^{2}.

Note that
\mathcal{S}_{b}
is the projectivisation of the classical Schwarzenberger vector bundle
\kappa_{*}\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-b-1,0)
introduced in
[
Sch61
]
. Moreover, there is a natural family of lines of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
whose preimage by
\mathcal{S}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{b}
for each
b\geq 0
(by Lemma
4.2.5
(1)
). This explains the shift in the notation.

The families of Umemura
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}
, introduced by Umemura in
[
Ume88
, § 10]
, need a bit more notation to be described. This is done in §
3.6
. The
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\mathcal{V}_{1}^{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a family of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles such that
\Autz(\mathcal{V}_{b})
is maximal and birationally conjugate to certain
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b,c})
, but with an action on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
fixing a point; see Lemma
5.5.1
for the precise relation between the two families.

Remark 1.2.8
.

Let us mention that the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles that appear in Theorem
A
are toric varieties (families (a) and (b)) or minimal smooth compactifications of
\SL_{2}/H
with
H\subseteq\SL_{2}
a finite subgroup (families (c), (d), and (e) and certain members of families (a) and (b)). Here we call
minimal smooth compactification
of
\SL_{2}/H
an
\SL_{2}
-equivariant embedding
\SL_{2}/H\hookrightarrow X
such that
X
is a smooth projective variety and any
\SL_{2}
-equivariant birational morphism
X\to X^{\prime}
, with
X^{\prime}
smooth, is an isomorphism; these
\SL_{2}
-threefolds were studied and classified by Nakano in
[
Nak89
]
(over
\mathbb{C}
) as an application of Mori theory. The finite subgroups of
\SL_{2}(\mathbb{C})
are well known: there are the cyclic groups, the binary dihedral groups, and the binary polyhedral groups. When
H
is non-cyclic,
\SL_{2}/H
admits a unique minimal smooth compactification, namely the Fano threefolds
V_{5}
,
V_{22}^{MU}
, and the smooth quadric
Q_{3}
for the binary polyhedral groups (see
[
Nak89
, Proposition 2.5]
), and the Schwarzenberger
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles or
\mathbb{P}^{2}
for the binary dihedral groups (see
[
Nak89
, Theorem 2.8]
). But when
H
is cyclic,
\SL_{2}/H
admits several non-isomorphic minimal smooth compactifications including the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles corresponding to families (c) and (e) in Theorem
A
(see
[
Nak89
, Theorem 2.31]
for the complete list).

1.3. Comparison with Umemura’s classification

Each of the maximal connected algebraic subgroups of
\Bir(\mathbb{P}^{3}{C})
given in
[
Ume85
, Theorem 2.1]

acts on some Mori fibre spaces (as explained in
[
Ume88
]
). The cases (P1), (P2), (E1), (E2) correspond to Fano threefolds, the cases (J10), (J12) to Del Pezzo fibrations (
\mathbb{P}^{2}
-bundles and quadric fibrations), and all other cases (J1)–(J9), (J11) correspond to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles. We now explain to which of the cases
(a)
–
(d)
of Theorem
A
these correspond.

Note that
\mathcal{F}^{b,c}_{a}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}(-bs_{a}+cf))\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
(see Definition
3.1.1
) and that
\mathcal{P}_{b}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(b)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-b))\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
(see Definition
4.1.1
).

(J1) is
\Autz(\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
and is thus given by
\mathcal{P}_{0}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

(J2) is
\Autz(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
and is thus given by
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{0,0}\to\mathbb{F}_{0}
.

(J3) is
\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{m}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
, with
m\geq 2
, which is given either by
\mathcal{F}_{m}^{0,0}\to\mathbb{F}_{m}
or by
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{0,m}\to\mathbb{F}_{0}
, which is square isomorphic to
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,0}\to\mathbb{F}_{0}
(but not isomorphic as a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle).

(J4) is
\Autz(\PGL_{3}/B)\simeq\PGL_{3}
and is thus equal to
\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{1})
, as
\mathcal{S}_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
\mathbb{P}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and
\PGL_{3}/B\to\PGL_{3}/P
are isomorphic, by Remark
4.2.8
.

(J5) is
\Autz(\PGL_{2}/D_{2n})
, with
n\geq 4
, and is thus equal to
\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})
, with
b=n-1\geq 3
, by Remark
4.2.7
.

(J6) is
\Autz(L_{m,n})
, where
L_{m,n}\to\mathbb{F}_{0}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle of bidegree
(m,n)
, with
m\geq 2
,
n\leq-2
(see
[
Ume85
, Theorem 2.1(J6)]
and
[
Ume88
, § 5]
). It thus corresponds to
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,-n}\to\mathbb{F}_{0}
, given in
(a)
.

(J7) is equal to
\Autz(\mathrm{J}_{m})=\Autz(\mathrm{J}^{\prime}_{m})
, with
m\geq 2
, where the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\mathrm{J}_{m}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
defined in
[
Ume88
, § 6]
is a compactification of
\mathrm{J}_{m}^{\prime}
of
[
Ume85
, Theorem 2.1(J7)]
, isomorphic to
\mathcal{P}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
with
m=b
, given in
(b)
.

(J8) is
\Autz(L_{m,n})
, where
L_{m,n}\to\mathbb{F}_{0}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle of bidegree
(m,n)
, with
m\geq n\geq 1
(see
[
Ume85
, Theorem 2.1(J8)]
and
[
Ume88
, § 7]
). It thus corresponds to
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,n}\to\mathbb{F}_{0}
, which is square isomorphic to
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{n,m}\to\mathbb{F}_{0}
, both given in
(a)
.

(J9) is
\Autz(\mathrm{F}_{m,n}^{\prime})
, for some integers
m>n\geq 2
(see
[
Ume85
, Theorem 2.1(J9)]
), and is also equal to
\Autz(\mathrm{F}_{m,n}^{k})
for each integer
k\geq\lfloor{m}/{n}\rfloor
, where
\mathrm{F}_{m,n}^{k}=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{n}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{n}}(-ks_{-n}-mf))=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{n}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{n}}(-ks_{n}+(nk-m)f))=\mathcal{F}_{n}^{k,nk-m}\to\mathbb{F}_{n}
, given in
(a)
.

(J11) is
\Autz(\mathrm{E}_{m}^{\prime\,l})
, where
m=1
,
l\geq 3
or
m,l\geq 2
(see
[
Ume85
, Theorem 2.1(J11)]
). Then
\mathrm{E}_{m}^{\prime\,l}
admits a family of compactifications given in
[
Ume88
, § 10]
and depending on a parameter
j\geq l
; these compactifications correspond to the Umemura bundles
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
, with
a=m
,
b=j
,
c=(j-l)m+2
.

Let us note that the family
(e)
in Theorem
A
was overlooked in the work of Umemura. These have to correspond to maximal algebraic subgroups of
\Bir(\mathbb{P}^{3})
and should appear in
[
Ume88
, § 10]
.

Some of the elements of our list do not appear in
[
Ume85
,
Ume88
]
. This is sometimes because these do not correspond to maximal algebraic subgroups of
\Bir(\mathbb{P}^{3})
, as we can embed the groups in larger groups of automorphisms of Mori fibre spaces, or it is because they are equivalent to other elements of the list, by a birational map not preserving the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle structure. This is for instance the case of
\mathcal{S}_{2}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
, which is
\Aut(\mathcal{S}_{2})
-equivariantly birational to
\mathbb{P}^{3}
, or of
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{0,1}\simeq\mathbb{F}_{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, which is not maximal as it is equivariantly birational to
\mathbb{P}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. There are many other cases, which are studied in
[
BFT21
]
.

1.4. Overview of the proof

Starting with a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\hat{\pi}\colon\hat{X}\to\hat{S}
over a smooth projective rational surface
\hat{S}
, there is a birational morphism
\eta\colon\hat{S}\to S
, where
S
is a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{a}
or the projective plane
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Applying Lemma
2.3.2
, we obtain a square birational map
\psi\colon(\hat{X},\hat{\pi})\dashrightarrow(X,\pi)
, unique up to isomorphism. Moreover,
\psi
is
\Autz(X)
-equivariant. We then need to study
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over Hirzebruch surfaces or over the projective plane.

Section
3
concerns the case of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over Hirzebruch surfaces
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
, with
a\geq 0
. We denote by
\tau_{a}\colon\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle structure on
\mathbb{F}_{a}
, and we study the surfaces
S_{p}=(\tau_{a}\pi)^{-1}(p)
with
p\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The situation is described by Proposition
3.2.2
; we explain it now. There is an integer
b\geq 0
such that
S_{p}\simeq\mathbb{F}_{b}
for a general
p\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
. If this holds for all points of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we in fact have a
\mathbb{F}_{b}
-bundle
\tau_{a}\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
: we say that there is no
jumping fibre
. If a special point
p\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
is such that
S_{p}\simeq\mathbb{F}_{b^{\prime}}
for some
b^{\prime}\neq b
, then
b^{\prime}>b
and we can blow up the exceptional curve of
S_{p}\simeq\mathbb{F}_{b^{\prime}}
and contract the strict transform of
S_{p}
, in an
\Autz(X)
-equivariant way. After finitely many steps, we reduce to the case where there is no jumping fibre.

We then associate to any
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
with no jumping fibres two integers
b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
b\geq 0
, and
c\leq 0
if
b=0
. The integer
b
is the one such that
\tau_{a}\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a
\mathbb{F}_{b}
-bundle, and the integer
c
can be seen either with the transition function of
\pi:X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
or using exact sequences associated to the rank
2
vector bundle corresponding to
\pi
, with the following definition (Corollary
3.2.3
shows the equivalence between the two points of view).

Definition 1.4.1
.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
a,b\geq 0
and
c\leq 0
if
b=0
.
We say that a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}

has numerical invariants
(a,b,c)
if it is the projectivisation of a rank
2
vector bundle

\mathcal{E}
which fits in a short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}\to\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}(-bs_{a}+cf)\to 0,

where
f,s_{a}\subset\mathbb{F}_{a}
are a fibre and a section of self-intersection
a
of
\tau_{a}\colon\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

In Proposition
3.3.1
, we show that a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
with numerical invariants
(a,b,c)
has no jumping fibre and that every
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
with no jumping fibre has numerical invariants
(a,b,c)
, for some uniquely determined integers
b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
, with
b\geq 0
, and
c\leq 0
if
b=0
. The above numerical invariants are thus really invariant under isomorphisms.

We then prove (see Corollary
3.3.7
) that every
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
with numerical invariants
(a,b,c)
is decomposable if
b=0
or
c<2
, and construct a moduli space
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{b,c}
, which is isomorphic to a projective space (see Remark
3.3.9
), parametrising the non-decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
with numerical invariants
(a,b,c)
when
b\geq 1
and
c\geq 2
(see Corollary
3.3.8
).

The group
\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{a})
acts naturally on this moduli space, via an algebraic action, which is detailed in §
3.4
. Using this action, we are able to describe the geometry of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
(see Proposition
3.7.4
). We prove in particular that if no surface
S_{p}
(with the notation above) is invariant by
\Autz(X)
, then
\pi
is isomorphic to a decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle, to an Umemura
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle, or to a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, which is obtained by pulling back the Schwarzenberger bundle
\mathcal{S}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
via the double cover
\kappa\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
defined above (see Lemma
4.2.4
); the last two cases correspond to natural elements of the moduli spaces
\mathcal{M}_{a}^{b,c}
fixed by
\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{a})
.

In the case
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and in the case where a fibre
S_{p}
is invariant, we can reduce to the case of decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{F}_{a}
(again by Proposition
3.7.4
).

Section
4
concerns
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. Despite the fact that the geometry of such bundles is quite rich and complicated (see
e.g.

[
OSS11
]
for an overview when
\mathrm{k}=\mathbb{C}
), our approach allows us to give a quite simple proof in this case, using the work done in §
3
for
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over Hirzebruch surfaces. We first study the Schwarzenberger
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\mathcal{S}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
(see Lemmas
4.2.1
,
4.2.4
, and
4.2.5
and Corollary
4.2.2
). We then take a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
and denote by
H\subset\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})
the image of
\Autz(X)
. If
H
fixes a point, we blow up the fibre of this point and reduce our study to the case of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{F}_{1}
. Otherwise, using the structure of
\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, we see that either
H=\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})=\PGL_{3}
or
H\subset\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},C)=\{g\in\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})\mid g(C)=C\}
, for some smooth curve
C
which is a line or a conic (see Lemma
4.3.3
). The case where
C
is a line cannot happen (see Proposition
4.3.4
), and the case where
C
is a conic corresponds to the Schwarzenberger case; this is proven by using the double cover
\kappa\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
of Definition
1.2.7
and the results on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over Hirzebruch surfaces. The case where
H=\PGL_{3}
corresponds to the decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
or to the special Schwarzenberger
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\mathcal{S}_{1}
, isomorphic to the projectivised tangent bundle
\mathbb{P}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
. Again, this is proven by a reduction to the case of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles of
\mathbb{F}_{1}
(studied in §
3
) by blowing up a point of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.

In §
5
, we prove Theorems
A
and
B
, which achieves our classification. Once we have reduced our study to decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{F}_{a}
or
\mathbb{P}^{2}
or to Umemura or Schwarzenberger
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles (see Proposition
5.1.1
), we study birational maps of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles between elements of these four families, which are in fact obtained by elementary links centred at invariant curves (see Lemma
5.3.1
), square isomorphisms, and special contractions from
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{F}_{1}
to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
(see Remark
5.2.1
). The study of the possible links is made on each family, by describing the possible invariant curves. These are naturally contained in the preimage of invariant curves of the surface
S
over which we take the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles and are most of the time obtained by a negative curve in the fibres of smooth rational curves.

1.5. Acknowledgments.

We are grateful to the anonymous referee for the careful reading of a previous version of this paper and for their helpful comments.
We would like to thank Michel Brion, Paolo Cascini, Ciro Ciliberto, Daniele Faenzi, Enrica Floris, Anne-Sophie Kaloghiros, Massimiliano Mella, Lucy Moser-Jauslin, Boris Pasquier, Francesco Russo, and Jean Vallès for interesting discussions related to this work. Thanks to Mateusz Michalek for indicating the proof of the combinatoric Lemma
3.4.4
to us. Thanks also to the MathOverflow community for its help regarding the proofs of Lemmas
3.7.2
and
4.3.3
.

2. Preliminaries

All the results in § 2 are valid over an algebraically closed field
\mathrm{k}
of arbitrary characteristic.

2.1. Blanchard’s lemma

We recall a result due to Blanchard
[
Bla56
, § I.1]
in the setting of complex geometry, whose proof has been adapted to the setting of algebraic geometry by Brion, Samuel, and Uma.

Lemma 2.1.1

(
cf.

[
BSU13
, Proposition 4.2.1]
)
.

Let
f\colon X\to Y
be a proper morphism between algebraic varieties such that
f_{*}(\mathcal{O}_{X})=\mathcal{O}_{Y}
. If a connected algebraic group
G
acts on
X
, then there exists a unique action of
G
on
Y
such that
f
is
G
-equivariant.

2.2. Resolution of indeterminacies

The next result implies that if
\pi:X\to S
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over a singular surface, then there exists an
\Autz(X)
-equivariant square birational map
(X,\pi)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
, with
\pi^{\prime}:X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over a smooth surface. Therefore, it is enough to consider the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over smooth surfaces to determine all the maximal automorphism groups
\Autz(X)
in the sense of Definition
1.2.3
.

Lemma 2.2.1
.

Let
\pi\colon X\to S
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over a singular projective surface
S
, and let
G\subset\Autz(X)
be a connected algebraic subgroup. Then there exist a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\pi^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
, equipped with a
G
-action, and
G
-equivariant birational morphisms
\eta\colon S^{\prime}\to S
and
\hat{\eta}\colon X^{\prime}\to X
such that
\eta
is a resolution of singularities and the diagram below is cartesian; in particular,
{\hat{\eta}}^{-1}G\hat{\eta}
is a subgroup of
\Autz(X^{\prime})
.

\textstyle{X^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{\eta}}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{S}

Proof.

By Lemma
2.1.1
, the group
G
acts biregularly on
S
.
Since
G
is connected, we can solve the singularities of
S
in a
G
-equivariant way by repeatedly alternating between normalization and blowing-up of the singular points (see for instance
[
Art86
]
). We denote by
\eta\colon S^{\prime}\to S
a
G
-equivariant resolution of singularities of
S
and define
\pi^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}:=X\times_{S}S^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
to be the pull-back of
\pi
along
\eta\colon S^{\prime}\to S
. The pull-back of a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle along a morphism of schemes is again a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle (indeed, all the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles that we consider are Zariski locally trivial).
Also, since
S^{\prime}\to S
is
G
-equivariant,
G
acts on
X^{\prime}
, and
\hat{\eta}:X^{\prime}\to X
is
G
-equivariant. The last statement follows from the fact that
\eta
, and thus
\hat{\eta}
, is birational.
∎

In the following, we will only consider the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over smooth surfaces; in particular, we will not describe the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
X\to S
over singular surfaces such that
\Autz(X)
is maximal. There are many such
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles, obtained from those of Theorem
A
by contracting the negative curve on a Hirzebruch surface onto a singular point. The obtained threefolds have, however, singularities which are of codimension
2
, so it is natural to avoid them when working in birational geometry with the classical minimal model program (where varieties have terminal singularities). We also recall that a
\mathbb{P}^{n}
-bundle over a smooth variety is isomorphic to the projectivisation of a rank
n+1
vector bundle over this variety (see
e.g.

[
Har77
, Exercise II.7.10(c)]
); this well-known fact will be used implicitly throughout the rest of the article.

2.3. The descent lemma

The following simple observation will be often used later. It explains that
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\pi:X\to S
over smooth projective surfaces are uniquely determined by a description on an open subset with finite complement. This is for instance useful over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
or Hirzebruch surfaces, where then only the restriction of
\pi
to two open subsets isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}
is needed to describe the whole
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle.

Lemma 2.3.1
.

Let
S
be a smooth projective surface, let
\Omega\subset S
be a finite set, and let
g\in\Aut(S)
be an automorphism that satisfies
g(\Omega)=\Omega
. Let
\pi_{1}\colon X_{1}\to S
and
\pi_{2}\colon X_{2}\to S
be two
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles, and let
\hat{g}\colon X_{1}\dashrightarrow X_{2}
be a birational map that restricts to an isomorphism
(\pi_{1})^{-1}(S\setminus\Omega)\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}(\pi_{2})^{-1}(S\setminus\Omega)
and satisfies
\pi_{2}\hat{g}=\pi_{1}g
. Then
\hat{g}
is an isomorphism of varieties
X_{1}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}X_{2}
:

\textstyle{X_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\hat{g}}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{1}}
\textstyle{X_{2}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{2}}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{S\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

In particular, if
g
is the identity, then
\hat{g}
is an isomorphism of
\,\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles.

Proof.

It suffices to take a point
p_{1}\in{}_{1}
and to show that
\hat{g}
is a local isomorphism around every point of the curve
(\pi_{1})^{-1}(p_{1})\subset X_{1}
. We denote by
U_{1}\subset S
an open neighbourhood of
p_{1}
and write
U_{2}=g(U_{1})
for its image, which is an open neighbourhood of
p_{2}=g(p_{1})\in\Omega
.
By shrinking
U_{1}
, we can assume that
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
are trivial
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
, respectively. The birational map
\hat{g}
is then described by

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}U_{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}&\dashrightarrow&U_{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\\
\left(x,\begin{bmatrix}u\\
v\end{bmatrix}\right)&\mapsto&\left(g(x),M(x)\cdot\begin{bmatrix}u\\
v\end{bmatrix}\right),\\
\end{array}

where
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}(S))
. Since
\mathrm{k}(S)
is the function field of
\mathcal{O}_{p_{1}}(S)
, which is a UFD (because
S
is smooth), we can multiplying
M
with an element of
\mathrm{k}(S)
and obtain a matrix
M^{\prime}
whose entries are all in
\mathcal{O}_{p_{1}}(S)
and do not have a common factor. Denote by
f\in\mathcal{O}_{p_{1}}(S)
the determinant of
M^{\prime}
. If
f
does not vanish at
p_{1}
, it is invertible in
\mathcal{O}_{p_{1}}(S)
, and
\hat{g}
yields an isomorphism
U^{\prime}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\to g(U^{\prime})\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, where
U^{\prime}\subset U
corresponds to the open subset where
f\neq 0
. This yields the result since
p_{1}\in U^{\prime}
.

It remains to show that
f
cannot vanish at
p_{1}
. Indeed, otherwise the zero set of
f
yields a curve
C
of
S
passing through
p_{1}
, and there is then a curve
\hat{C}\subset C
with
\pi_{1}(\hat{C})=C
on which the map
\hat{g}
is not defined (since the matrix
M^{\prime}
is unique up to multiplication with an element of
\mathcal{O}_{p_{1}}(S)^{*}
and since at least one of the entries of
M^{\prime}
is not divisible by
f
).
∎

We can then prove the following descent lemma, already invoked in the introduction.

Lemma 2.3.2

(Descent lemma)
.

Let
\eta\colon\hat{S}\to S
be a birational morphism between two smooth projective surfaces. Let
U\subset S
and
\hat{U}\subset\hat{S}
be two maximal open subsets such that
\eta
induces an isomorphism
\hat{U}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}U
and
\Omega=S\setminus U
is finite, and let
\hat{\pi}\colon\hat{X}\to\hat{S}
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle.

Then, there exist a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\pi\colon X\to S
and a birational map
\psi\colon\hat{X}\dashrightarrow X
such that
\eta\hat{\pi}=\pi\psi

(\psi
is a square birational map over
\eta)
and such that
\psi
induces an isomorphism
\hat{\pi}^{-1}(\hat{U})\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\pi^{-1}(U)
. Moreover,
\psi
is unique, up to composition by an isomorphism of
\,\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles at the target, and
\psi
is
\Autz(\hat{X})
-equivariant, which means that
\psi\Autz(\hat{X})\psi^{-1}
is a subgroup of
\Autz(X)
.

Proof.

Writing
G=\Autz(\hat{X})
, Lemma
2.1.1
implies that
\hat{\pi}
and
\eta
are
G
-equivariant for some unique biregular action of
G
on
\hat{S}
and
S
.

If
\eta
is an isomorphism, everything is trivial. Otherwise,
\eta
is the blow-up of finitely many points, so
\eta
restricts to an isomorphism
\hat{U}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}U
, where
U\subset S
is an open subset,
\Omega=S\setminus U
is a finite set, and
\hat{U}=\eta^{-1}(U)
. We denote by
j
the inclusion
U\hookrightarrow S
.

Let
\mathcal{E}\to\hat{S}
be a rank
2
vector bundle such that
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\simeq\hat{X}
, and let
\mathcal{E}_{\hat{U}}\to\hat{U}
be its restriction over
\hat{U}
.
Then
\mathcal{E}_{\hat{U}}\to\hat{U}
identifies with
\eta_{*}\mathcal{E}_{\hat{U}}\to U
, and we can consider the reflexive hull
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}=(j_{*}(\eta_{*}\mathcal{E}_{\hat{U}}))^{\vee\vee}
of the coherent sheaf
j_{*}(\eta_{*}\mathcal{E}_{\hat{U}})
on
S
. By
[
Har80
, Corollary 1.4]
, the reflexive sheaf
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}
is locally free, and thus
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}\to S
is a rank
2
vector bundle that extends (uniquely)
\eta_{*}\mathcal{E}_{\hat{U}}
. Writing
X=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime})
, we obtain a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
X\to S
that extends (uniquely) the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\mathbb{P}(\eta_{*}\mathcal{E}_{\hat{U}})\to U
.

Since
\eta:\hat{S}\to S
is
G
-equivariant and the open subsets
\hat{U}
and
U
are
G
-stable, the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\hat{X}\rightarrow\hat{S}
is
G
-equivariantly birational to
{X}_{U}(=\mathbb{P}(\eta_{*}\mathcal{E}_{\hat{U}}))\to U
. It remains to apply Lemma
2.3.1
to see that the
G
-action on
{X}_{U}
extends to
X
.
∎

Remark 2.3.3
.

If
\eta
is the blow-up of a point
p\in S
and
E=\eta^{-1}(p)\subset\hat{S}
is the exceptional curve, the birational map
\psi
can be described as follows (according to
[
Mel02
, § 5.7.4, p. 700]
), depending on the surface
Z=\hat{\pi}^{-1}(E)\simeq\mathbb{F}_{a}
: if
a=0
, then
\psi
is a birational morphism whose restriction to
Z\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the “other projection”; if
a>0
, then
\psi
is given by the anti-flip of the exceptional curve of
Z
followed by the contraction of the strict transform of
Z
, isomorphic to the weighted projective plane
\mathbb{P}(1,1,a)
, onto a smooth point.

2.4. Hirzebruch surfaces

In the following, we will always use the following coordinates for Hirzebruch surfaces, which are the analogue of the standard coordinates for
\mathbb{P}^{2}=(\mathbb{A}^{3}\setminus\{0\})/\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Definition 2.4.1
.

Let
a\in\mathbb{Z}
. The
a
-th Hirzebruch surface

\mathbb{F}_{a}
is defined to be the quotient of
(\mathbb{A}^{2}\setminus\{0\})^{2}
by the action of
(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{2}
given by

\begin{array}[]{ccc}(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{2}\times(\mathbb{A}^{2}\setminus\{0\})^{2}&\to&(\mathbb{A}^{2}\setminus\{0\})^{2}\\
\big((\mu,\rho),(y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1})\big)&\mapsto&(\mu\rho^{-a}y_{0},\mu y_{1},\rho z_{0},\rho z_{1}).\end{array}

The class of
(y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1})
will be written
[y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]
. The projection

\tau_{a}\colon\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1},\ \ [y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[z_{0}:z_{1}]

identifies
\mathbb{F}_{a}
with
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(a)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}})
as a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

The disjoint sections
s_{-a},s_{a}\subset\mathbb{F}_{a}
given by
y_{0}=0
and
y_{1}=0
have self-intersection
-a
and
a
, respectively. The fibres
f\subset\mathbb{F}_{a}
given by
z_{0}=0
and
z_{1}=0
are linearly equivalent and of self-intersection
0
. We moreover get
\Pic(\mathbb{F}_{a})=\mathbb{Z}f\bigoplusop\displaylimits\mathbb{Z}s_{-a}=\mathbb{Z}f\bigoplusop\displaylimits\mathbb{Z}s_{a}
since
s_{a}\sim s_{-a}+af
.

Remark 2.4.2
.

The surface
\mathbb{F}_{a}
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{-a}
via
[y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[y_{1}:y_{0};z_{0}:z_{1}]
, so we will most of the time choose
a\geq 0
.

Let us recall the classical structure of the automorphism groups of Hirzebruch surfaces. The description of Definition
2.4.1
allows one to present all automorphisms in a simple way.

Remark 2.4.3
.

If
a=0
, then
\mathbb{F}_{a}\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and the natural action of
(\GL_{2})^{2}
on
(\mathbb{A}^{2}\setminus\{0\})^{2}
yields a surjective group homomorphism
(\GL_{2})^{2}\to\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{0})\simeq(\PGL_{2})^{2}
(see
e.g.

[
BSU13
, Corollary 4.2.7]
for the last isomorphism). All automorphisms are then of the form

[y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[\alpha y_{0}+\beta y_{1}:\gamma y_{0}+\delta y_{1};\alpha^{\prime}z_{0}+\beta^{\prime}z_{1}:\gamma^{\prime}z_{0}+\delta^{\prime}z_{1}].

The action of
\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{0})
on
\mathbb{F}_{0}
is then homogeneous (one single orbit). Moreover,
\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{0})=\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{0})\rtimes\langle\iota\rangle
, with
\iota\colon[y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[z_{0}:z_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}]
.

Remark 2.4.4
.

If
a\geq 1
, then the curve
s_{-a}
given by
y_{0}=0
is the unique section of negative self-intersection of
\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
and is thus invariant. Denoting by
\mathrm{k}[z_{0},z_{1}]_{a}\subset\mathrm{k}[z_{0},z_{1}]
the vector space of homogeneous polynomials of degree
a
, one gets an action of
\mathrm{k}[z_{0},z_{1}]_{a}\rtimes\GL_{2}
on
\mathbb{F}_{a}
via

[y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[y_{0}:y_{1}+y_{0}p(z_{0},z_{1});\alpha z_{0}+\beta z_{1}:\gamma z_{0}+\delta z_{1}],

which yields an exact sequence

1\rightarrow\mu_{a}\to\mathrm{k}[z_{0},z_{1}]_{a}\rtimes\GL_{2}\rightarrow\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{a})\rightarrow 1,

where
\mu_{a}\subset\GL_{2}
is the cyclic group of homotheties
\alpha
with
\alpha^{a}=1
.
To prove the surjectivity of the morphism
\mathrm{k}[z_{0},z_{1}]_{a}\rtimes\GL_{2}\rightarrow\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{a})
, it suffices to consider the elements acting trivially on the basis of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
(by Blanchard’s lemma, since
\GL_{2}
itself acts transitively on the basis
\mathbb{P}^{1}
). One then restricts such an automorphism to the two charts
z_{0}\neq 0
and
z_{1}\neq 0
and obtains two
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-automorphisms of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
that fix
y_{0}=0
,
i.e.
two triangular elements of
\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[z_{0}/z_{1}])
and
\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[z_{1}/z_{0}])
, and that must coincide on the intersection. A simple calculation then shows that it comes from an element of
\mathrm{k}[z_{0},z_{1}]_{a}\rtimes\GL_{2}
as above. In particular, this implies that
\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{a})
is connected. Moreover,
\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{a})=\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{a})
acts on
\mathbb{F}_{a}
with two orbits, namely
s_{-a}
and its complement.

Remark 2.4.5
.

It follows from Remarks
2.4.3
and
2.4.4
that for each
a\geq 0
, the morphism
\tau_{a}\colon\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
yields a surjective group homomorphism

\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{a})\twoheadrightarrow\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{1})\simeq\PGL_{2}.

In particular,
\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{a})
acts transitively on the set of fibres of
\tau_{a}\colon\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
.

We also recall the following easy observation, that we will need further on.

Lemma 2.4.6
.

Let
a\geq 0
, and let
\mathcal{E}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
be a rank
2
vector bundle that fits into an exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\iota}}{{\to}}\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(-a)\to 0.

Then we have an isomorphism from
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
to
\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
which sends the section corresponding to
\iota(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}})
onto
s_{-a}
.

Proof.

We trivialise
\mathcal{E}
on the two open subsets of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
given by
\{[1:z]\mid z\in\mathbb{A}^{1}\}
and
\{[z:1]\mid z\in\mathbb{A}^{1}\}
, change coordinates so that
\iota(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}})
corresponds to
x_{0}=0
, and get a transition

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}&\dashrightarrow&\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\\
\big((x_{0},x_{1}),z\big)&\dashrightarrow&\big((z^{a}x_{0},x_{1}+f(z)),\frac{1}{z}\big)\end{array}

for some
f\in\mathrm{k}[z,z^{-1}]
.
We can then compose at the source and target with some automorphisms of the form
((x_{0},x_{1}),z)\mapsto((x_{0},y_{1}+h_{i}(z)),z)
for polynomials
h_{1},h_{2}\in\mathrm{k}[z]
. This replaces
f
by
f+h_{1}(z)+h_{2}(z^{-1})z^{a}
. Since
a\geq 0
, we can thus replace
f
with
0
. The projectivisation of
\mathcal{E}
is then isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{a}
, by sending
([x_{0}:x_{1}],z)\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
onto
[x_{0}:x_{1};1:z]
and
[x_{0}:x_{1};z:1]=[x_{0}z^{a}:x_{1};1:z^{-1}]
, on both charts.
∎

3.
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
-bundles over Hirzebruch surfaces

Most of the results in §
3
are valid over an algebraically closed field
\mathrm{k}
of arbitrary characteristic. More precisely, §§
3.1
-
3.3
are valid in arbitrary characteristic, the results in §
3.4
are all valid in arbitrary characteristic, but the proof of Corollary
3.4.6
that we give is shortened a bit by using the characteristic zero assumption (see Remark
3.4.7
); §§
3.5
and
3.6
are also valid in arbitrary characteristic different from
2
, except Lemma
3.5.5
(3)
, which fails if and only if
\mathrm{char}(\mathrm{k})
divides
b+1
(see Remark
3.5.6
). In §
3.7
we must assume that the ground field
\mathrm{k}
has characteristic zero as Lemma
3.7.2
and Proposition
3.7.4
are false over any field of positive characteristic (see Remarks
3.7.3
and
3.7.5
).

3.1. Decomposable
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
-bundles over Hirzebruch surfaces

As for Hirzebruch surfaces
\mathbb{F}_{a}
(Definition
2.4.1
), one can give global coordinates on decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{F}_{a}
.

Definition 3.1.1
.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
. We define
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
to be the quotient of
(\mathbb{A}^{2}\setminus\{0\})^{3}
by the action of
(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{3}
given by

\begin{array}[]{ccc}(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{3}\times(\mathbb{A}^{2}\setminus\{0\})^{3}&\to&(\mathbb{A}^{2}\setminus\{0\})^{3}\\
\big((\lambda,\mu,\rho),(x_{0},x_{1},y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1})\big)&\mapsto&(\lambda\mu^{-b}x_{0},\lambda\rho^{-c}x_{1},\mu\rho^{-a}y_{0},\mu y_{1},\rho z_{0},\rho z_{1}).\end{array}

The class of
(x_{0},x_{1},y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1})
will be written
[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]
. The projection

\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a},\quad[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]

identifies
\mathcal{F}^{b,c}_{a}
with

\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}(bs_{a})\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}(cf)\big)=\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}(-bs_{a}+cf)\big)

as a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{F}_{a}
, where
s_{a},f\subset\mathbb{F}_{a}
are given by
y_{1}=0
and
z_{1}=0
.

Moreover, every fibre of the composed morphism
\mathcal{F}^{b,c}_{a}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
given by the
z
-projection is isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{b}
, and the restrictions of
\mathcal{F}^{b,c}_{a}
on the curves
s_{-a}
and
s_{a}
given by
y_{0}=0
and
y_{1}=0
are isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{c}
and
\mathbb{F}_{c-ab}
, respectively.

As for Hirzebruch surfaces, one can reduce to the case
a\geq 0
without changing the isomorphism class, by exchanging
y_{0}
and
y_{1}
. We then observe that the exchange of
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
yields an isomorphism
\mathcal{F}^{b,c}_{a}\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\mathcal{F}^{-b,-c}_{a}
. We will assume
a,b\geq 0
most of the time from here on. If
b=0
, we can moreover assume
c\leq 0
.

Remark 3.1.2
.

Every decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{F}_{a}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
for some
b,c\in\mathbb{Z},b\geq 0
. We can moreover assume
b\geq 0
, and
c\leq 0
if
b=0
, since
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\simeq\mathcal{F}_{a}^{-b,-c}
.

The
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
has numerical invariants
(a,b,c)
(see Definition
1.4.1
). As we will see later (Remark
3.3.2
), these numerical invariants are indeed invariant under isomorphism. This will show that the isomorphism classes of decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over Hirzebruch surfaces are parametrised by these invariants.

Remark 3.1.3
.

All decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
are toric varieties, with an action given by the map
[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[x_{0}:\alpha x_{1};y_{0}:\beta y_{1};z_{0}:\gamma z_{1}]
.

Remark 3.1.4
.

We have two open embeddings

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}&\hookrightarrow&\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\\
\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z\big)&\mapsto&\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};1:z]\big)\\
\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z\big)&\mapsto&\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z:1]\big)\end{array}

over which the
\mathbb{F}_{b}
-bundle
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is trivial, with a transition function

\begin{array}[]{c}\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z\big)\mapsto\left([x_{0}:x_{1}z^{c};y_{0}z^{a}:y_{1}],\frac{1}{z}\right).\end{array}

Lemma 3.1.5
.

Let
a,b\geq 0
and
c\in\mathbb{Z}
. The morphism
\pi\colon\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
yields a surjective group homomorphism

\rho\colon\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})\twoheadrightarrow\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{a}).

Proof.

The existence of
\rho
is given by Lemma
2.1.1
. The fact that it is surjective can be seen by observing that every automorphism
g\in\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{a})
comes from an automorphism of
(\mathbb{A}^{2}\setminus\{0\})^{2}
(see Remarks
2.4.3
and
2.4.4
), so we can extend the action to
(\mathbb{A}^{3}\setminus\{0\})^{2}
and then
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
by doing nothing on
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
.
∎

Remark 3.1.6
.

For each
i,j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
\mathrm{k}[y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1}]_{i,j}\subset\mathrm{k}[y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1}]
the space of homogeneous polynomials of bidegree
(i,j)
, where the variables
y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1}
are of bidegree
(1,-a),(1,0),(0,1),(0,1)
. The group of automorphisms of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles of
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
identifies with the (connected) group

\left\{\begin{bmatrix}p_{1,0,0}&p_{2,-b,c}\\
p_{3,b,-c}&p_{4,0,0}\end{bmatrix}\in\PGL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1}])\ \middle|\ \begin{array}[]{l}p_{k,i,j}\in\mathrm{k}[y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1}]_{i,j}\\
\mbox{for }k=1,\ldots,4.\end{array}\right\}

whose action on
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
is as follows:

[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}:z_{0};z_{1}]\mapsto[x_{0}p_{1}+x_{1}p_{2}:x_{0}p_{3}+x_{1}p_{4};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}].

This can be seen directly from the global description of
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
in Definition
3.1.1
and by using trivialisations on open subsets isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}
.

3.2. Removal of jumping fibres

Removing a fibre into a Hirzebruch surface, we get an open subset isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. It is then natural to study the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
in order to get a local description of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over Hirzebruch surfaces.

Lemma 3.2.1
.

Let
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle, and let
\tau\colon\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
be the first projection. Then, there exist an integer
b\geq 0
and a dense open subset
U\subset\mathbb{A}^{1}

(
both uniquely determined by
\pi)
such that the following hold:

(1)

The generic fibre of the morphism
{\tau\pi}\colon X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
is isomorphic to the Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{b}
.

(2)

There exists a commutative diagram

\textstyle{(\tau\pi)^{-1}(U)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{U\times\mathbb{F}_{b}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{1}\times\tau_{b}}
\textstyle{U\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}

where
\mathrm{pr}_{1}\times\tau_{b}
sends
(u,x)
onto
(u,\tau_{b}(x))
and
\tau_{b}\colon\mathbb{F}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the standard
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle.

(3)

For each
p\in\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus U
, the fibre
(\tau\pi)^{-1}(p)
is isomorphic to the Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{b+2\epsilon}
for some positive integer
\epsilon
.

(4)

For each
p\in\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus U
, we can blow up the exceptional section of
(\tau\pi)^{-1}(p)
and contract the strict transform of
(\tau\pi)^{-1}(p)
; this replaces
X
by another
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
X^{\prime}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
as above, with a new open subset
U^{\prime}
which is equal either to
U
or to
U\cup\{p\}
. After finitely many such steps, we get the case where
U^{\prime}=\mathbb{A}^{1}
, corresponding to a trivial
\mathbb{F}_{b}
-bundle.

Proof.

We choose two open subsets of
V_{0},V_{1}\subset\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}
via

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\begin{array}[]{rcccccc}\iota_{0}\colon&\mathbb{A}^{2}&\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}&V_{0}\subset\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\\
&(x,y)&\to&\big(x,[1:y]\big)\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{rccc}\iota_{1}\colon&\mathbb{A}^{2}&\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}&V_{1}\subset\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}.\\
&(x,y)&\to&\big(x,[y:1]\big)\end{array}\end{array}

The restriction of
\pi
to the open subsets
V_{0}
and
V_{1}
yield
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{A}^{2}
, which are then trivial, by the Quillen–Suslin theorem (see
[
Lan02
, Theorem XXI.3.7]
) combined with the fact that a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{A}^{2}
is isomorphic to the projectivisation of a rank
2
vector bundle over
\mathbb{A}^{2}
since
\mathbb{A}^{2}
is a smooth variety. This gives the existence of isomorphisms
\varphi_{i}\colon\pi^{-1}(V_{i})\to\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, for
i=0,1
, such that
\iota_{i}\mathrm{pr}_{1}\varphi_{i}=\pi
, where
\mathrm{pr}_{1}\colon\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{A}^{2}
is the first projection.
The isomorphisms
\varphi_{i}
are uniquely determined, up to composing at the target and the source with elements of
\PGL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[\mathbb{A}^{2}])\subset\Aut(\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
.
We then write the transition function
\varphi_{1}(\varphi_{0})^{-1}
as

\big((x,y),[u:v]\big)\mapsto\big((x,y^{-1}),[\alpha_{11}(x,y)u+\alpha_{12}(x,y)v:\alpha_{21}(x,y)u+\alpha_{22}(x,y)v]\big),

where
A=\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{11}&\alpha_{12}\\
\alpha_{21}&\alpha_{22}\end{bmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[x,y,y^{-1}])
. Note that the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\pi
is determined by the equivalence class of
A
modulo
A\sim\lambda MAM^{\prime}
, where
\lambda\in\mathrm{k}[x,y,y^{-1}]^{*}=\mathrm{k}^{*}y^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
M\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[x,y^{-1}])
,
M^{\prime}\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[x,y])
. In particular, we can multiply
A
with an element of
\mathrm{k}^{*}
and assume
\det(A)\in y^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

Working over the field
\mathrm{k}(x)
, we get a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{1}_{\mathrm{k}(x)}
, which is therefore isomorphic to a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{b}
, with
b\geq 0
(this follows from the fact that a vector bundle on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
is decomposable over any field). This yields two matrices
B\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}(x)[y^{-1}])
and
C\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}(x)[y])
such that

B^{-1}AC=D=\begin{bmatrix}y^{m}&0\\
0&y^{n}\end{bmatrix}

for some integers
m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
with
m-n=b
, and yields part
(1)
. Since
\det(B),\det(C)\in\mathrm{k}(x)^{*}
and
\det(A),\det(D)\in y^{\mathbb{Z}}
, we get
\det(A)=\det(D)=y^{m+n}
, so
\det(B)=\det(C)\in\mathrm{k}(x)^{*}
.

Writing the equality
AC=BD
, we can multiply both
B
and
C
with the same element of
\mathrm{k}(x)^{*}
and assume
B\in\mathrm{Mat}_{2,2}(\mathrm{k}[x,y^{-1}])
,
C\in\mathrm{Mat}_{2,2}(\mathrm{k}[x,y])
, and
(B(\lambda),C(\lambda))\neq(0,0)
for each
\lambda\in\mathrm{k}
, where
B(\lambda),C(\lambda)\in\mathrm{Mat}_{2,2}(\mathrm{k}(y))
are obtained by replacing
x
by
\lambda
in
B,C
.

We denote by
\mathcal{Z}\subset\mathbb{A}^{1}
the zero set of
\det(B)=\det(C)\in\mathrm{k}[x]
. On the open set
U=\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\mathcal{Z}
, the matrices
B
and
C
correspond to automorphisms of
U\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
on the two charts, respectively, so we have a trivial
\mathbb{F}_{b}
-bundle on
U
. In particular, if
\mathcal{Z}=\emptyset
, then
\tau\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
is a trivial
\mathbb{F}_{b}
-bundle, and the proof is over. We can thus prove the result by induction on the degree of the polynomial
\det(B)
.

Suppose that
\lambda\in\mathcal{Z}
is such that the fibre
(\tau\pi)^{-1}(\lambda)
is a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{\tilde{b}}
. Hence,
A(\lambda)
corresponds to the transition function of
\mathbb{F}_{\tilde{b}}
, which means that
\tilde{B}^{-1}A(\lambda)\tilde{C}=\begin{bmatrix}y^{\tilde{m}}&0\\
0&y^{\tilde{n}}\end{bmatrix}
with
\tilde{B}\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[y^{-1}])
,
\tilde{C}\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[y])
,
\tilde{m},\tilde{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
, and
\tilde{m}-\tilde{n}=\tilde{b}\geq 0
. Computing the determinant yields
m+n=\tilde{m}+\tilde{n}
. Writing
\epsilon=\tilde{m}-m=n-\tilde{n}
, we then get
\tilde{b}-b=(\tilde{m}-\tilde{n})-(m-n)=2\epsilon
. Replacing
A,B,C
with
\tilde{B}^{-1}A\tilde{C}
,
(\tilde{B})^{-1}B
,
(\tilde{C})^{-1}C
, we keep the equation
AC=BD
, do not change the degree of
\det(B)=\det(C)
or the set
\mathcal{Z}
, and can then assume
A(\lambda)=\begin{bmatrix}y^{\tilde{m}}&0\\
0&y^{\tilde{n}}\end{bmatrix}
. Writing
B(\lambda)=\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{11}&\beta_{12}\\
\beta_{21}&\beta_{22}\end{bmatrix}
yields

C(\lambda)=A(\lambda)^{-1}B(\lambda)D=\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{11}y^{m-\tilde{m}}&\beta_{12}y^{n-\tilde{m}}\\
\beta_{21}y^{m-\tilde{n}}&\beta_{22}y^{n-\tilde{n}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{11}y^{-\epsilon}&\beta_{12}y^{-b-\epsilon}\\
\beta_{21}y^{b+\epsilon}&\beta_{22}y^{\epsilon}\end{bmatrix}.

(a)
If the first column of
B(\lambda)
is zero, then so is the first column of
C(\lambda)
. Writing
\Delta=\begin{bmatrix}x-\lambda&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}
, we get
B=B^{\prime}\Delta
and
C=C^{\prime}\Delta
for some
B^{\prime},C^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Mat}_{2,2}(\mathrm{k}[x,y,y^{-1}])
. Replacing
B
and
C
by
B^{\prime}
and
C^{\prime}
does not change the equation
AC=BD
, since
D
commutes with
\Delta
, and decreases the degree of
\det(B).
A similar argument works if the second column of
B
is zero.

(b)
If
\epsilon<0
, then
-b-\epsilon=\epsilon-\tilde{b}<0
, so the second column of
B(\lambda)
and
C(\lambda)
is zero since
B(\lambda)\in\mathrm{Mat}_{2,2}(\mathrm{k}[y^{-1}])
and
C(\lambda)\in\mathrm{Mat}_{2,2}(\mathrm{k}[y])
; we then apply
(a)
.

(c)
If
\epsilon=b=0
, then
B(\lambda)=C(\lambda)\in\mathrm{Mat}_{2,2}
. There thus exists an
R\in\GL_{2}
such that the first column of
B(\lambda)R
is zero. We can replace
B,C
with
BR,CR
, since
R
commutes with
D=y^{m}\cdot I=y^{n}\cdot I
, and reduce to case
(a)
.

(d)
If
\epsilon=0
and
b>0
, then
\beta_{12}=0
and
\beta_{22}\in\mathrm{k}
. If
\beta_{22}=0
, we do as above. If
\beta_{22}\neq 0
, we get
\beta_{11}=0
since
\det(B(\lambda))=0
, hence
B(\lambda)=\begin{bmatrix}0&0\\
\beta_{21}&\beta_{22}\end{bmatrix}
, so the first column of
B(\lambda)\cdot R
is zero, with
R=\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{22}&0\\
-\beta_{21}&1\end{bmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[y^{-1}])
. Writing
R^{\prime}=D^{-1}RD=\begin{bmatrix}\beta_{22}&0\\
-\beta_{21}y^{b}&1\end{bmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[y])
, we can replace
B
and
C
with
BR
and
CR^{\prime}
and get case
(a)
.

(e)
The last case is when
\epsilon>0
, which implies that
\tilde{b}=b+2\epsilon\geq b+2\geq 2
and that
\beta_{11}=\beta_{12}=0
.

After applying the steps above, we can assume that all elements of
\mathcal{Z}
give rise to case
(e)
. Writing
U=\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\mathcal{Z}
, this yields parts
(2)
and
(3)
.

It remains to show part
(4)
, by studying more carefully case
(e)
. Note that the fibre
x=\lambda
corresponds to the Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{\tilde{b}}
, with
\tilde{b}\geq 2
, and a special section corresponds to
u=0
in the charts
\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. The blow-up of the exceptional section, followed by the contraction of the strict transform of the surface
\mathbb{F}_{\tilde{b}}
, corresponds locally to

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}&\dashrightarrow&\mathbb{A}^{2}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\\
((x,y),[u:v])&\mapsto&((x,y),[u:(x-\lambda)v]).\end{array}

This replaces the transition matrix
A
with
A^{\prime}=\Delta^{-1}A\Delta
, where
\Delta=\begin{bmatrix}x-\lambda&0\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}:

A=\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{11}&\alpha_{12}\\
\alpha_{21}&\alpha_{22}\end{bmatrix},\quad A^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}\alpha_{11}&\frac{\alpha_{12}}{x-\lambda}\\
(x-\lambda)\alpha_{21}&\alpha_{22}\end{bmatrix}.

Note that the new transition
A^{\prime}
still belongs to
\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[x,y,y^{-1}])
since
A(\lambda)
is diagonal, which implies that
\alpha_{12}
and
\alpha_{21}
are multiples of
x-\lambda
.

Moreover, the first line of
B(\lambda)
and
C(\lambda)
is zero, so we can write
B=\Delta B^{\prime}
and
C=\Delta C^{\prime}
for some
B^{\prime},C^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Mat}_{2,2}(\mathrm{k}[x,y,y^{-1}])
. The blow-up replaces
A
by
A^{\prime}
, and we can replace
B,C
with
B^{\prime},C^{\prime}
since
A^{\prime}C^{\prime}=(\Delta^{-1}A\Delta)(\Delta^{-1}C)=\Delta^{-1}BD=B^{\prime}D
. This process decreases the degree of
\det(B)=\det(C)
; we get a trivial
\mathbb{F}_{b}
-bundle after finitely many steps.
∎

As a consequence of Lemma
3.2.1
, we get the following result.

Proposition 3.2.2

(Removal of jumping fibres)
.

Let
a\geq 0
, and let
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle. There exist an integer
b\geq 0
and a dense open subset of
U\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}
such that
(\tau_{a}\pi)^{-1}(p)
is a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{b}
for each
p\in U
. Moreover, we have:

(1)

If
U=\mathbb{P}^{1}
, then
\tau_{a}\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is an
\mathbb{F}_{b}
-bundle which is trivial on every affine open subset of
\,\mathbb{P}^{1}
. In this case, we say that
\pi
has no jumping fibre
.

(2)

If one fibre
(\tau_{a}\pi)^{-1}(\{p\})
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{c}
for some
c\neq b
, then
c-b
is a positive even integer
(
we say that
\tau_{a}^{-1}(\{p\})
is a
jumping fibre
)
, and the blow-up of the
(
unique
)
exceptional section of
\,\mathbb{F}_{c}
followed by the contraction of the strict transform of
\,\mathbb{F}_{c}
gives an
\Autz(X)
-equivariant birational map
X\dashrightarrow X^{\prime}
to another
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{F}_{a}
. After finitely many such steps, one gets case
(1)
.

Proof.

For each
p\in\mathbb{P}^{1}
, we have a commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{1}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{F}_{a}\setminus\tau_{a}^{-1}(\{p\})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{a}}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{p\}\hbox to0.0pt{.\hss}}

We can thus apply Lemma
3.2.1
on each affine subset
\mathbb{P}^{1}\setminus\{p\}
and get the result.
∎

Another consequence of Lemma
3.2.1
is the following description.

Corollary 3.2.3
.

Let
\pi\colon X\to S=\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle, let
\tau\colon S\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
be the first projection, and let
b\geq 0
. The following conditions are equivalent:

(1)

The fibre
(\tau\pi)^{-1}(p)
is a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{b}
for each
p\in\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

(2)

There exists a commutative diagram

\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{F}_{b}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{pr}_{1}\times\tau_{b}}
\textstyle{S=\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}}

where
\mathrm{pr}_{1}\times\tau_{b}
sends
(u,x)
on
(u,\tau_{b}(x))
.

(3)

The
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
X\to S
is the projectivisation of a rank
2
vector bundle
\mathcal{E}
which fits in a short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{S}\to\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{O}_{S}(-bs)\to 0,

where
s
is a fibre of
\,\mathrm{pr}_{2}\colon S\to\mathbb{P}^{1}

(
which satisfies
\Pic(S)=\mathbb{Z}s)
.

Proof.

The implication
(1)

\Rightarrow

(2)
is given by
Proposition
3.2.2
(1)
. To get
\ref{PA1e2}\Rightarrow\ref{PA1e3}
, we observe that
(2)
yields an isomorphism between the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
X\to\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}\big(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}\bigoplusop\displaylimits\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{1}}(b)\big)\to\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. To get
(3)

\Rightarrow

(1)
,
we restrict the exact sequence to each fibre of
\tau\colon S\to\mathbb{A}^{1}
and apply Lemma
2.4.6
.
∎

3.3. Moduli spaces of
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles over
\mathbb{F}_{a}
with no jumping fibre

The following proposition associates to every
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over a Hirzebruch surface with no jumping fibre three unique invariants
(a,b,c)
, called
numerical invariants
in Definition
1.4.1
. The integer
a
is given by the Hirzebruch surface over which the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle is taken. The integer
b
is given by the generic fibre of the projection to
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, which is a Hirzebruch surface
\mathbb{F}_{b}
. The last integer
c
can be seen using exact sequences or using transition functions, as the following result explains.

Proposition 3.3.1
.

Let
a\geq 0
, and let
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle.

(1)

For all integers
b\geq 0
and
c\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following are equivalent:

(
i
)

The variety
X
is the gluing of two copies of
\,\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
along
\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\}
by the automorphism
\nu_{c,P}\in\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\})
given by

\begin{array}[]{c}\nu_{c,P}\colon([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z)\mapsto\left([x_{0}:x_{1}z^{c}+x_{0}P(y_{0},y_{1},z);y_{0}z^{a}:y_{1}],\frac{1}{z}\right)\end{array}

for some
P\in\mathrm{k}[y_{0},y_{1},z,z^{-1}]
, homogeneous of degree
b
in
y_{0},y_{1}
, such that
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
sends
([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z)\in\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
onto, respectively,

[y_{0}:y_{1};1:z]\in\mathbb{F}_{a}
and
[y_{0}:y_{1};z:1]\in\mathbb{F}_{a}
on the two charts.

(
ii
)

The
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
is the projectivisation of a rank
2
vector bundle

\mathcal{E}
which fits in a short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}\to\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}(-bs_{a}+cf)\to 0,

where
f,s_{a}\subset\mathbb{F}_{a}
are given by
y_{1}=0
and
z_{1}=0
.

(2)

If there exists a
b\geq 0
such that the preimage of each fibre of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\tau_{a}\colon\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{b}

(
no jumping fibre
)
, then there is an integer
c\in\mathbb{Z}
such that the above properties are satisfied. If
b>0
, the integer
c
is unique. If
b=0
, then
\lvert c\rvert
is unique and
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
is isomorphic to the decomposable bundles
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
and
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,-c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
.

Proof.

We first prove
(1)(i )

\Rightarrow

(1)(ii )
.
The section
x_{0}=0
being invariant by the transition function, one can see
X
as the projectivisation of a rank
2
vector bundle

\mathcal{E}
which fits in a short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}\to\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}(\beta s_{a}+\gamma f)\to 0

for some integers
\beta,\gamma\in\mathbb{Z}
. To compute these numbers, we take the two open subsets
U_{0},U_{1}\subset\mathbb{F}_{a}
isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}
via

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\begin{array}[]{rcccccc}\theta_{0}\colon&\mathbb{A}^{2}&\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}&U_{1}\subset\mathbb{F}_{a},\\
&(y,z)&\to&[1:y;1:z]\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{rccc}\theta_{1}\colon&\mathbb{A}^{2}&\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}&U_{2}\subset\mathbb{F}_{a}\\
&(y,z)&\to&[y:1;z:1]\end{array}\end{array}

and observe that the vector bundle has a transition function of the form

(x_{0},x_{1},y,z)\mapsto\left(y^{b}z^{-c}x_{0},x_{1}+x_{0}P(1,y,z)z^{-c},\frac{z^{a}}{y},\frac{1}{z}\right),

which yields
\beta=-b
and
\gamma=c
.

We then prove
(2)
. It follows from Proposition
3.2.2
that the pull-back on
X
of the two open subsets
V_{0},V_{1}\subset\mathbb{F}_{a}
given by

\begin{array}[]{lllll}\begin{array}[]{rcccccc}\theta_{0}\colon&\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}&\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}&V_{0}\subset\mathbb{F}_{a},\\
&([y_{0}:y_{1}],z)&\to&[y_{0}:y_{1};1:z]\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{rccc}\theta_{1}\colon&\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}&\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}&V_{1}\subset\mathbb{F}_{a}\\
&([y_{0}:y_{1}],z)&\to&[y_{0}:y_{1};z:1]\end{array}\end{array}

are isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\mathbb{F}_{b}
. The transition function on
\mathbb{F}_{a}
being given by
([y_{0}:y_{1}],z)\dashrightarrow([z^{a}y_{0}:y_{1}],z^{-1})
, we get a transition function
\psi\in\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\})
of the form

\begin{array}[]{ccccccc}\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}&\dashrightarrow&\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\\
\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z\big)&\mapsto&\big([f_{0}(x_{0},x_{1},y_{0},y_{1},z):f_{1}(x_{0},x_{1},y_{0},y_{1},z);z^{a}y_{0}:y_{1}],\frac{1}{z}\big)\end{array}

for some
f_{0},f_{1}\in\mathrm{k}[x_{0},x_{1},y_{0},y_{1}][z,z^{-1}]
. The isomorphism class of
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
(as in Definition
1.2.1
) is then determined by
\psi
, up to composition at the source and target with automorphisms of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
.

If
b=0
, then
\psi
is of the form

([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z)\mapsto([\alpha(z)x_{0}+\beta(z)x_{1};\gamma(z)x_{0}+\delta(z)x_{1};z^{a}y_{0}:y_{1}],z^{-1})
,
where
A=\begin{bmatrix}\alpha(z)&\beta(z)\\
\gamma(z)&\delta(z)\end{bmatrix}\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[z,z^{-1}])
. The isomorphism class of
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
is then given by the matrix
A
, up to replacing
A
by
\lambda BAC
, where
B\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[z^{-1}])
,
C\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[z])
, and
\lambda\in\mathrm{k}^{*}
. The class of
A
modulo this replacement corresponds to a vector bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{1}
, which is therefore equivalent to a decomposable one, with a diagonal matrix
A
. We then get an integer
c\in\mathbb{Z}
, unique up to sign, such that
\psi
is of the form

\begin{array}[]{c}\psi\colon\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z\big)\mapsto\left([x_{0}:x_{1}z^{c};y_{0}z^{a}:y_{1}],\frac{1}{z}\right).\end{array}

This shows that
X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}=\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}

(see Remark
3.1.4
) and also to
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,-c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
.

If
b>0
, then
\psi
is of the form

([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z)\mapsto([x_{0}:\mu(z)x_{1}+x_{0}P(y_{0},y_{1},z);z^{a}y_{0}:y_{1}],z^{-1})
,
where
\mu(z)\in\mathrm{k}[z,z^{-1}]^{*}=\mathrm{k}^{*}\cdot z^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
P(y_{0},y_{1},z)
is a homogeneous polynomial of degree
b
in
y_{0}
,
y_{1}
, with coefficients in
\mathrm{k}[z,z^{-1}]
. Applying a diagonal automorphism at the target, we can assume
\mu(z)=z^{c}
for some
c\in\mathbb{Z}
. We then observe that
c
is unique since the transition function is determined up to automorphisms of
\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
at the target and the source, which do not change
c
.

It remains to prove
(1)(ii )

\Rightarrow

(1)(i )
.
The inclusion
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}
corresponds to a section of
X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
. We restrict the exact sequence to a fibre
f
and get
0\to\mathcal{O}_{f}\to\mathcal{E}_{f}\to\mathcal{O}_{f}(-b)\to 0.
The corresponding section then needs to be the exceptional section of
\mathbb{F}_{b}
, unique if and only if
b>0
(see Lemma
2.4.6
).
The preimage of each fibre of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\tau_{a}\colon\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is then isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{b}

(
no jumping fibre
)
. We then apply
(2)
and get
(1)(i )
for some unique integer
c^{\prime}
. The calculation made in the proof of
(1)(i )

\Rightarrow

(1)(ii )
implies that
c=c^{\prime}
since the inclusion
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}
corresponds to the section
x_{0}=0
.
∎

Remark 3.3.2
.

Proposition
3.3.1
shows that two
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles with different numerical invariants (see Definition
1.4.1
) are not isomorphic. The numerical invariants defined in Definition
1.4.1
, which correspond to the integers
(a,b,c)
of Proposition
3.3.1
(where
c\leq 0
when
b=0
), are then really invariant under isomorphisms.

In particular,
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
is the unique isomorphism class of decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles with invariants
(a,b,c)
. (This follows from Remark
3.1.2
).

As a direct consequence of Proposition
3.3.1
, we obtain the following corollary (which is well known over the field of complex numbers; see
e.g.

[
ABM12
, § 2.2]
).

Corollary 3.3.3
.

Let
a,b\geq 0
, and let
\mathcal{E}
be a rank
2
vector bundle over
\mathbb{F}_{a}
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

There exists an exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}(ds_{-a}+rf)\to\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}(d^{\prime}s_{-a}+r^{\prime}f)\to 0

for some integers
d,d^{\prime},r,r^{\prime}
such that
b=d-d^{\prime}
.

(2)

The preimage by
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
of each fibre of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\tau_{a}\colon\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{b}
.

Moreover, the extension in
(1)
is unique if
b>0
.

Proof.

(1)

\Rightarrow

(2)
The inclusion
\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{F}_{a}}(ds_{-a}+rf)\hookrightarrow\mathcal{E}
corresponds to a section of
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E})\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
. We restrict the exact sequence to a fibre
f
and get
0\to\mathcal{O}_{f}(d)\to\mathcal{E}_{f}\to\mathcal{O}_{f}(d^{\prime})\to 0
, which yields the same
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle as
0\to\mathcal{O}_{f}\to\mathcal{E}_{f}\to\mathcal{O}_{f}(-b)\to 0
. The corresponding section then needs to be the exceptional section of
\mathbb{F}_{b}
(see Lemma
2.4.6
), which is unique if
b>0
.

(2)

\Rightarrow

(1)
This follows from Proposition
3.3.1
(2)
.
∎

Notation 3.3.4
.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
, with
a,b\geq 0
, and
c\leq 0
if
b=0
. For each

P\in\mathrm{k}[y_{0},y_{1},z,z^{-1}]_{b}=\{f\in\mathrm{k}[y_{0},y_{1},z,z^{-1}]\mbox{ homogeneous of degree $b$ in }y_{0},y_{1}\}
,

we denote by
Z_{a}^{b,c,P}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle given by
the gluing of two copies of
\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
along
\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\}
by the automorphism
\nu_{c,P}\in\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\})
given by

\nu_{c,P}\colon\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z\big)\mapsto\left([x_{0}:x_{1}z^{c}+x_{0}P(y_{0},y_{1},z);y_{0}z^{a}:y_{1}],\frac{1}{z}\right),

such that
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
sends
([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z)\in\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
onto, respectively,

[y_{0}:y_{1};1:z]\in\mathbb{F}_{a}
and
[y_{0}:y_{1};z:1]\in\mathbb{F}_{a}
on the two charts.

Remark 3.3.5
.

Proposition
3.3.1
shows that every
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{F}_{a}
with no jumping fibre is isomorphic to
Z_{a}^{b,c,P}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
for some
b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
with
b\geq 0
, and
c\leq 0
if
b=0
, and some
P\in\mathrm{k}[y_{0},y_{1},z,z^{-1}]_{b}
, and that it has numerical invariants
(a,b,c)
(see Definition
1.4.1
). Moreover,
Z_{a}^{b,c,0}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
(see Remark
3.1.4
).

Lemma 3.3.6
.

Let
\pi\colon Z_{a}^{b,c,P}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
and
\pi^{\prime}\colon Z_{a}^{b^{\prime},c^{\prime},P^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
be two
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles as in Notation
3.3.4
, with
b\geq 1
. Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

The
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\pi\colon Z_{a}^{b,c,P}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
and
\pi^{\prime}\colon Z_{a}^{b^{\prime},c^{\prime},P^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
are isomorphic.

(2)

We have
b^{\prime}=b
and
c^{\prime}=c
, and there exist
\lambda\in\mathrm{k}^{*}
and
Q_{1},Q_{2}\in\mathrm{k}[y_{0},y_{1},z]
homogeneous of degree
b
in
y_{0},y_{1}
such that

P^{\prime}=\lambda P+Q_{1}(y_{0},y_{1},z)z^{c}+Q_{2}(y_{0}z^{a},y_{1},z^{-1})
.

Proof.

If
b\neq b^{\prime}
, then the two
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles are not isomorphic since the preimages of the fibres of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\tau_{a}\colon\mathbb{F}_{a}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
are not isomorphic. We can thus assume
b^{\prime}=b
.

The two
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles are obtained by gluing two copies of
\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
over
\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\}
by
\nu_{c,P},\nu_{c^{\prime},P^{\prime}}\in\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\})
(see Notation
3.3.4
). The
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles are thus isomorphic if and only if
\nu_{c^{\prime},P^{\prime}}=\alpha\nu_{c,P}\beta
for some automorphisms
\alpha
,
\beta
of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
. Since
b\geq 1
, such elements are of the form

\begin{array}[]{rcccccc}\theta_{\lambda,Q}\colon&\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}&\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}&\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\\
&\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z\big)&\mapsto&\big([x_{0}:\lambda x_{1}+x_{0}Q(y_{0},y_{1},z);y_{0}:y_{1}],z\big),\end{array}

where
\lambda\in\mathrm{k}^{*}
and
Q\in\mathrm{k}[y_{0},y_{1},z]_{b}
. The composition
\theta_{\lambda_{2},Q_{2}}\nu_{c,P}\theta_{\lambda_{1},Q_{1}}
yields
