(2:1)
-cover
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{b}\to\mathcal{S}_{b}
is
\PGL_{2}
-equivariant. Hence, by Remark
3.5.7
,
\mathcal{S}_{b}
has an open
\PGL_{2}
-orbit, say
\PGL_{2}/F
, where
F
is a finite subgroup scheme that fits into an exact sequence
0\to H\to F\to\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}\to 0
with
H=\left\{\begin{bmatrix}\alpha&0\\
0&\alpha^{-1}\end{bmatrix};\ \alpha^{m}=1\right\}
. Actually, using (
4.1
), an explicit computation of the stabiliser of a general point in the
\mathbb{P}^{1}
-fibre over
[0:1:0]\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
yields that
F
is conjugate to
\left\langle H,\ \begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-1&0\end{bmatrix}\right\rangle
; see
[
Ume88
, Lemma 4.5]
for details.

Remark 4.2.8
.

Writing
X=\mathbb{P}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
, Lemma
4.2.5
shows that the action of
\Autz(X)
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
yields an isomorphism
\Autz(X)\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\PGL_{3}
. This classical observation can also be seen as follows. Let
B
be a Borel subgroup of
G=\PGL_{3}
, and let
P
be a maximal parabolic subgroup of
G
containing
B
. Then the structure morphism
X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
identifies with the projection
G/B\to G/P
(see
e.g.

[
IP99
, Example 2.1.8]
).
Therefore, by
[
Dem77
, Theorem 1]
, we have
\Autz(X)=\Autz(G/B)=G
.

4.3. Reduction to
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{P}^{1}}
-bundles of the four families

We first show that the Schwarzenberger and decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
are those which have large automorphism groups.

Lemma 4.3.1
.

Let
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle, let
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the conic of equation
Y^{2}=4XZ
, and let
H\subset\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})
be the image of
\Autz(X)
. Then, the following hold:

(1)

H=\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})
if and only if
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a decomposable bundle or is isomorphic to the projectivised tangent bundle.

(2)

\PGL_{2}\simeq\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},\Gamma)\subset H
if and only if
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a decomposable bundle or is isomorphic to a Schwarzenberger bundle
\mathcal{S}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
for some
b\geq 1
.

Proof.

If
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a decomposable bundle, then
H=\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})
(see Lemma
4.1.2
). The same holds if
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{S}_{1}\simeq\mathbb{P}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
(see Corollary
4.2.2
and Lemma
4.2.5
). If
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{S}_{b}
for some
b\geq 2
, then
H=\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},\Gamma)
(see Lemma
4.2.5
(2)
).

It then remains to assume
\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},\Gamma)\subset H
and deduce from it that
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is decomposable or isomorphic to
\mathcal{S}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
for some
b\geq 1
. We use the double cover
\kappa\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
ramified over
\Gamma
given in Definition
1.2.7
and define a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\hat{\pi}\colon\hat{X}=X\times_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. By construction, the image of
\Autz(\hat{X})\to\Autz(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
contains the group
\PGL_{2}
embedded diagonally, namely the subgroup of
\Autz(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
preserving the diagonal
\Delta
, the branch locus of
\kappa
. This implies that
\hat{\pi}\colon\hat{X}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is either a decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle or square isomorphic to
\hat{S}_{b}
for some
b\geq 1
(see Proposition
3.7.4
). In the latter case, the isomorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
that comes in the square has to preserve the diagonal and lifts to
\Autz(\hat{X})
(see Lemma
3.5.5
(2)
), so
\hat{\pi}\colon\hat{X}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is in fact isomorphic to
\hat{S}_{b}
. Denoting by
\sigma\in\Aut(\hat{X})
the involution induced by the automorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
exchanging the two factors, we find
X=\hat{X}/\sigma
.

We first assume that
\hat{X}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is a decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle, say
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m_{1},m_{2}}
. For
i=1,2
, the fibres of
\mathrm{pr}_{i}\circ\hat{\pi}\colon\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m_{1},m_{2}}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
are the Hirzebruch surfaces
\mathbb{F}_{\lvert m_{i}\rvert}
, where
\mathrm{pr}_{i}\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the
i
-th projection. As
\sigma
exchanges the fibres, we get
m_{1}=\pm m_{2}
. We now prove that up to conjugation by an isomorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles, we have one of the following situations for the action of
\sigma
on
\hat{X}
:

\begin{array}[]{ccccc}\sigma:&\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,-m}&\to&\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,-m}\\
&[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]&\mapsto&[x_{0}:x_{1};z_{0}:z_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],&m\geq 0;\\[4.30554pt]
\sigma:&\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,-m}&\to&\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,-m}\\
&[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]&\mapsto&[x_{0}:-x_{1};z_{0}:z_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],&m\geq 0;\text{ or }\\[4.30554pt]
\sigma:&\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,m}&\to&\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,m}\\
&[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]&\mapsto&[x_{1}:x_{0};z_{0}:z_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],&m\geq 0.\end{array}

If
m_{1}=m_{2}=0
, then
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{0,0}
is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{P}^{1})^{3}
, so
\sigma
is of the form
[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto

[\iota(x_{0}:x_{1});z_{0}:z_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}]
for some element
\iota\in\PGL_{2}
, which is either the identity or of order
2
. Applying conjugation, we get one of the above cases (the last two cases being conjugate).

We then assume
m_{1}=m_{2}=m>0
. The union of
-m
-curves in the fibres of
\mathrm{pr}_{i}\circ\hat{\pi}\colon\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,m}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
is then given by
x_{0}=0
and
x_{1}=0
. This implies that
\sigma
is of the form
[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto

[x_{1}:\xi x_{0};z_{0}:z_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}]
for some
\xi\in\mathrm{k}^{*}
. Conjugating by an automorphism
[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[\mu x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]
, we can assume
\xi=1
. This gives the third case.

The remaining cases are when
m_{1}=-m_{2}=m>0
. The union of
-m
-curves in the fibres of
\mathrm{pr}_{i}\circ\hat{\pi}
is then given by
x_{0}=0
, for
i=1,2
. This implies that
\sigma
is of the form
[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto

[x_{0}:\mu x_{1}+x_{0}P(y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1});z_{0}:z_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}]
, where
\mu\in\mathrm{k}^{*}
and
P
is of bidegree
m,m
in
y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1}
. As
\sigma
is an involution, we get
\mu=\pm 1
and
\mu P(y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1})+P(z_{0},z_{1},y_{0},y_{1})=0
. In both cases, conjugating by the automorphism
[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[x_{0}:x_{1}\pm\frac{1}{2}x_{0}P(y_{0},y_{1},z_{0},z_{1});y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]
, we can assume that
P=0
and obtain one of the first two cases.

We now study the three cases and show that only the first case can appear, in which case
X
is a decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
In the first case, we obtain that
X=\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,-m}/\sigma=\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(-m)\oplus\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
is a decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. In the second case, we consider
U:=[1:x;1:y;1:z]\simeq\mathbb{A}^{3}
, which is a
\sigma
-stable affine open subset of
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,-m}
. The corresponding action on
\mathbb{A}^{3}
is
(x,y,z)\mapsto(-x,z,y)
, which is conjugate to
(x,y,z)\mapsto(-x,-y,z)
. In the third case, we consider
V:=[x-1:x+1;1:y+z;1:-y+z]\simeq\mathbb{A}^{3}
, which is a
\sigma
-stable affine open subset of
\mathcal{F}_{0}^{m,m}
. The action is again given by
(x,y,z)\mapsto(-x,-y,z)
. In both cases, the quotient is singular since the invariant algebra is
\mathrm{k}[x^{2},xy,y^{2},z]=\mathrm{k}[X_{1},X_{2},X_{3},X_{4}]/(X_{1}X_{3}-(X_{2})^{2})
, which is impossible since
X
is smooth.

It remains to study the case where
\hat{X}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is isomorphic to
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
. By Lemma
4.2.4
,
\hat{X}
is equal to the pull-back of
\mathcal{S}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
, so there is an involution
\sigma^{\prime}\in\Aut(\hat{X})
, acting on
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
as the exchange of the two factors
\tau\in\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
, such that
\hat{X}/\sigma^{\prime}=\mathcal{S}_{b}
. Since every automorphism of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is trivial (see Lemma
3.5.5
(2)
),
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
is the unique lift of
\tau
, which shows that
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{S}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
.
∎

Remark 4.3.2
.

If
\mathrm{k}=\mathbb{C}
, then Lemma
4.3.1
(1)
is a particular case of a classical result due to Van de Ven
[
VdV72
, Theorem]
, and Lemma
4.3.1
(2)
follows from a result due to Vallès
[
Val00
, Theorem]
.

The following result is well known to specialists. Here we give a very short proof relying on recent results by Laurent.

Lemma 4.3.3
.

If
G\subsetneq\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})=\PGL_{3}
is a proper connected algebraic subgroup that does not fix any point of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, then one of the following holds:

(1)

G=\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},\Gamma)\simeq\PGL_{2}
, where
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a smooth conic; or

(2)

G
is a subgroup of
\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},L)\simeq\GL_{2}\ltimes\mathrm{k}^{2}
, where
L\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a line, equal either to
\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},L)
or to
\SL_{2}\ltimes\mathrm{k}^{2}\subset\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},L)
.

Moreover, in both cases,
G
acts on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
with two orbits: the smooth conic
\Gamma
in case
(1)
or the line
L
in case
(2)
, and its open complement.

Proof.

If
G
is solvable, then it acts on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
with a fixed point by the Borel fixed-point theorem (see
[
Hum75
, § 21.2, Theorem]
). If
G
is semisimple, then by
[
Lau18
, Proposition 3.4.1]
, the only possibility is case
(1)
. If
G
is not solvable nor semisimple, then by
[
Lau18
, Proposition 3.4.4]
, the only possibility is case
(2)
.
The last sentence of the statement can be checked with a direct computation.
∎

Proposition 4.3.4
.

Let
\pi\colon X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle. If
\,X
is not decomposable or isomorphic to a Schwarzenberger bundle
\mathcal{S}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
, for some
b\geq 1
, then there exists a
p\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
such that the fibre
f=\pi^{-1}(p)
is
\Autz(X)
-invariant. The blow-up
\eta\colon\hat{X}\to X
of
f
yields a commutative diagram with
\Autz(X)
-equivariant maps

\textstyle{\hat{X}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\scriptstyle{\hat{\pi}}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\mathbb{F}_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau}
\textstyle{\mathbb{P}^{2}\hbox to0.0pt{,\hss}}

where
\hat{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{1}
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{F}_{1}
and
\tau
is the blow-up of
p
.

Proof.

Let
H\subset\PGL_{3}
be the image of the natural group homomorphism
\Autz(X)\to\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})
. The assumption implies that
H
does not contain any group
\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},\Gamma)
for some smooth conic
\Gamma
(see Lemma
4.3.1
).

If
H
fixes a point
p\in\mathbb{P}^{2}
, we blow up simultaneously
p
in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
f=\pi^{-1}(p)
in
X
. Computing explicitly these blow-ups in a trivializing local chart containing
p_{0}
, we see that
\hat{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{1}
is again a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle. Moreover, since
p
and
f
are
\Autz(X)
-stable in
\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
X
, respectively, the group
\Autz(X)
acts on
\hat{X}
.

It remains to show that the case where no point of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
is fixed by
H
is impossible. By Lemma
4.3.3
, this case can only happen if
H=\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},L)\simeq\GL_{2}\ltimes(\mathrm{k}^{2},+)
or
H\simeq\SL_{2}\ltimes(\mathrm{k}^{2},+)\subsetneq\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},L)
, where
L\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a line. We take a point
p\in\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus L
and denote by
G_{0}
and
H_{0}
the subgroups of
G=\Autz(X)
and
H
stabilizing
p
(note that
H_{0}\simeq\GL_{2}
or
H_{0}\simeq\SL_{2}
). Blowing up the point
p
and its fibre yields a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\hat{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{1}
equipped with a
G_{0}
-action. As the group
H_{0}
acts transitively on
\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus(L\cup\{p\})\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}\setminus\{0\}
, it acts transitively on the exceptional divisor of the blow-up in
\mathbb{F}_{1}
, and thus
H_{0}
acts transitively on
\mathbb{P}^{1}
via the structure morphism
\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
. Therefore, by Proposition
3.7.4
, the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\hat{X}\to\mathbb{F}_{1}
is a decomposable or an Umemura
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle. In both cases, the natural group homomorphism
\hat{G}=\Autz(\hat{X})\to\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{1})
is onto (see Lemmas
3.1.5
and
3.6.3
), and so
H_{0}
acts transitively on the complement of the exceptional section of
\mathbb{F}_{1}
(see Remark
2.4.4
). Moreover, by Lemma
2.3.1
, the group
\hat{G}
identifies with a subgroup of
G
; in particular,
G
must act transitively on
\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus\{p\}
, contradicting the equality
H=\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},L)
.
∎

5. The classification

In this section, we first reduce to four families of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles, in Proposition
5.1.1
, which uses Propositions
3.7.4
and
4.3.4
and is thus valid only in characteristic zero. We then study equivariant square birational maps between the four families, in §§
5.2
–
5.7
;
the content of these subsections is valid over any algebraically closed field of characteristic different from
2
. We then apply the results obtained in §§
5.1
–
5.7
to get the main result in §
5.8
, valid only in characteristic zero.

5.1. Reduction to the four families

As a first step towards Theorem
A
, we prove the following.

Proposition 5.1.1
.

Let
\pi\colon X\to S
be a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over a smooth projective rational surface
S
.
There exists an
\Autz(X)
-equivariant square birational map
(X,\pi)\dashrightarrow(X^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
such that
(X^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
is isomorphic to one of the following:

(a)

a decomposable

\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle

\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}

\longrightarrow

\mathbb{F}_{a}

for some
a,b\geq 0
,
c\in\mathbb{Z}
;

(b)

a decomposable

\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle

\mathcal{P}_{b}

\longrightarrow

\mathbb{P}^{2}

for some
b\geq 0
;

(c)

an Umemura

\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle

\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}

\longrightarrow

\mathbb{F}_{a}

for some
a,b\geq 1,c\geq 2
;

(d)

a Schwarzenberger

\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle

\mathcal{S}_{b}

\longrightarrow

\mathbb{P}^{2}

for some
b\geq 1
.

Proof.

Using the descent lemma (Lemma
2.3.2
), we can assume
S=\mathbb{P}^{2}
or
S=\mathbb{F}_{a}
for some
a\geq 0
.

In the case where
S=\mathbb{F}_{a}
, we apply Proposition
3.7.4
to reduce to the case of decomposable or Umemura bundles.

In the case where
S=\mathbb{P}^{2}
, we apply Proposition
4.3.4
to reduce to the case of decomposable Schwarzenberger bundles, or to the case of
\mathbb{F}_{1}
, already studied.
∎

5.2. First links

Remark 5.2.1
.

We now study equivariant square birational maps from a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
X\to S
to another
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle

X^{\prime}\to S^{\prime}
, both in the above families.
The action being equivariant, we get a birational map
\eta\colon S\dashrightarrow S^{\prime}
which conjugates the image
H\subset\Autz(S)
of
\Autz(X)
to a subgroup of
\Autz(S^{\prime})
. Hence,
\eta
is a sequence of blow-ups of points fixed by
H
followed by a sequence of contractions of curves invariant by
H
. The nature of the pair
(H,S)
given above implies that no point of
S
is fixed and that the only
(-1)
-curve invariant by
H
is the exceptional curve of
\mathbb{F}_{1}
. We then only need to consider this case and study the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{P}^{2}
obtained (which is given by the descent lemma (Lemma
2.3.2
)) and square isomorphisms/birational maps of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles (doing nothing on
S
).

We then observe that no decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{F}_{1}
has a maximal group of automorphisms.

Lemma 5.2.2
.

For each
b\geq 0
and each
c\in\mathbb{Z}
, the rational map

\begin{array}[]{rccc}\varphi\colon&\mathcal{F}_{1}^{b,c}&\dashrightarrow&\mathcal{P}_{b-c}\\
&[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]&\mapsto&[x_{0}y_{0}^{c}:x_{1};y_{0}z_{0}:y_{1}:y_{0}z_{1}]\end{array}

is a square birational map above a birational morphism
\eta\colon\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
corresponding to the blow-up of
\,[0:1:0]
. Moreover,
\varphi\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{b,c})\varphi^{-1}\subsetneq\Autz(\mathcal{P}_{b-c})
.

Proof.

The birational morphism
\eta\colon\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
[y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[y_{0}z_{0}:y_{1}:y_{0}z_{1}]
is the blow-up of
[0:1:0]
, and by the construction of
\varphi
, we find that this is a square birational map above
\tau
. We then write
U=\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus\{[0:1:0]\}
and
\hat{U}=\tau^{-1}(U)\subset\mathbb{F}_{1}
and observe that
\varphi
induces an isomorphism
\hat{\pi}^{-1}(\hat{U})\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\pi^{-1}(U)
. By Lemma
2.3.2
,
\varphi
is the unique square birational map above
\tau
having this property (up to composition by an isomorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles at the target) and is
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{b,c})
-equivariant, which yields
\varphi\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{b,c})\varphi^{-1}\subset\Autz(\mathcal{P}_{b-c})
. We moreover have
\varphi\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{b,c})\varphi^{-1}\subsetneq\Autz(\mathcal{P}_{b-c})
since
\Autz(\mathcal{P}_{b-c})
acts transitively on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
(see Lemma
4.1.2
), but every element of
\varphi\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{1}^{b,c})\varphi^{-1}
acts on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
by fixing
[0:1:0]
.
∎

5.3. Reduction of birational maps to elementary links

We show here that every birational map of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles between the four types in Proposition
5.1.1
is a sequence of elementary links.

Lemma 5.3.1
.

Let
G
be a connected algebraic group acting on a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
X\to S
, let
H\subset\Autz(S)
be the image of
G
under this action, and assume either that no curve of
S
is invariant by
H
or that
(H,S)
is one of the following pairs:
(\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{a}),\mathbb{F}_{a})
with
a\geq 1
,
(\Autz(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1},\Delta),\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
, or
(\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},\Gamma),\mathbb{P}^{2})
, where
\Gamma=\{[X:Y:Z]\mid Y^{2}=4XZ\}\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
\Delta\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the diagonal.

If
\pi^{\prime}\colon X^{\prime}\to S
is a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle and
\varphi\colon X\dashrightarrow X^{\prime}
is a
G
-equivariant birational map of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
(
as in Definition
\ref{Defi:SquareEtc})
which is not an isomorphism, then we have a sequence of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\pi_{i}\colon X_{i}\to S
,
i=0,\ldots,n
, with
\pi_{0}=\pi
and
\pi_{n}=\pi^{\prime}
, and we can write
\varphi=\varphi_{n}\circ\cdots\circ\varphi_{1}
, where
\varphi_{i}\colon X_{i}\dashrightarrow X_{i+1}
is the blow-up of an irreducible curve
\ell_{i}\subset X_{i}
, followed by the contraction of the strict transform of
{\pi_{i}}^{-1}(\pi_{i}(\ell))
, and where
\pi_{i}|_{\ell_{i}}\colon\ell_{i}\to\pi_{i}(\ell_{i})
yields an isomorphism between
\ell_{i}
and either
s_{-a}\subset\mathbb{F}_{a}
with
a\geq 1
,
\Delta\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, or
\Gamma\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}.

Moreover, taking
n
minimal, the sequence
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{n}
is unique up to isomorphisms of
\,\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles.

Proof.

Taking an open subset
U\subset S
isomorphic to
\mathbb{A}^{2}
, the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
X
is trivial, so corresponds to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{2}
. On this chart, the birational map
\varphi
is of the form

\big([x_{0}:x_{1}],(u,v))\mapsto([a(u,v)x_{0}+b(u,v)x_{1}:c(u,v)x_{0}+d(u,v)x_{1}],(u,v)\big)

for some
a,b,c,d\in\mathrm{k}(u,v)
with
ad-bc\neq 0
. Choose
a,b,c,d\in\mathrm{k}[u,v]
with no common factor; the polynomials
a,b,c,d
are unique, up to multiplication by an element of
\mathrm{k}^{*}
. The zero locus of the determinant
P=ad-bc
thus corresponds exactly to the subset of
U
over which
\varphi
is not an isomorphism.

Denoting by
K\subset S
the subset over which
\varphi
is not an isomorphism, we find that
K
is a union of closed irreducible curves. The map
\varphi
being
G
-equivariant,
K
is invariant by
H
. The assumption made on
(H,S)
implies that either
K=\emptyset
, in which case
\varphi
is an isomorphism, or
(K,S)
is one of the three cases

(s_{-a},\mathbb{F}_{a})
,
(\Delta,\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})
, or
(\Gamma,\mathbb{P}^{2}).

In the three cases, we can choose, for each point
p\in K
, an open set
U\subset S
and an isomorphism
U\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\mathbb{A}^{2}
which sends
K\cap\mathbb{A}^{2}
onto the line
u=0
. Writing
\varphi
with
a,b,c,d\in\mathrm{k}[u,v]
as above, we find that
ad-bc=\lambda u^{n}
for some integer
n\geq 1
and
\lambda\in\mathrm{k}^{*}
. As
u
does not divide all polynomials
a,b,c,d
, the matrix
M(u,v)=\begin{bmatrix}a&b\\
c&d\end{bmatrix}
is such that
M_{0}=M(0,v)
has rank
1
. The ring
\mathrm{k}[v]
being a principal ideal domain,
we can use the Smith normal form and find
A,B\in\GL_{2}(\mathrm{k}[v])
(that are in fact products of elementary matrices since
\mathrm{k}[v]
is Euclidean) such that
AM_{0}B=\begin{bmatrix}e&0\\
0&0\end{bmatrix}
for some
e\in\mathrm{k}[v]\setminus\{0\}
. Replacing
M
with
AMB
, we then obtain
b=ub^{\prime}
and
d=ud^{\prime}
for some
b^{\prime},d^{\prime}\in\mathrm{k}[u,v]
and get
M=M^{\prime}R
with
M^{\prime}=\begin{bmatrix}a&b^{\prime}\\
c&d^{\prime}\end{bmatrix}
and
R=\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
0&u\end{bmatrix}
. The base locus of
\varphi
is then given, in these coordinates, by
u=0
and
x_{0}=0
, corresponding to a curve
\ell\subset X
such that
\pi
yields an isomorphism
\pi|_{\ell}\colon\ell\to K
. The blow-up of this curve followed by the contraction of the strict transform of the surface
\pi^{-1}(K)
is locally given by the matrix
R
. As
\det(M^{\prime})=\lambda u^{n-1}
, we proceed by induction and obtain the result.
∎

5.4. Links between decomposable bundles over Hirzebruch surfaces

Lemma 5.4.1
.

Let
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
with
a,b\geq 0
. The curves of
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
invariant by
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
are given as follows:

(1)

The curve
l_{00}
given by
x_{0}=y_{0}=0
is invariant if and only if
ab>0
or
ac<0
.

(2)

The curve
l_{10}
given by
x_{1}=y_{0}=0
is invariant if and only if
ac>0
.

(3)

No irreducible curve
\ell\subset\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
with
\ell\neq l_{00}
and
\ell\neq l_{10}
is invariant.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.1.5
, the morphism
\pi\colon\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
yields a surjective group homomorphism
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})\twoheadrightarrow\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{a})
. Hence, if
\ell\subset\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
is a curve invariant by
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
, then
\pi(\ell)=s_{-a}
and
a>0
(see Remarks
2.4.3
and
2.4.4
). Moreover,
\mathbb{G}_{m}
acts on
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
via
[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0},z_{1}]\mapsto[x_{0}:tx_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]
, so any point of
\ell
should satisfy
x_{0}=0
or
x_{1}=0
(otherwise we get a whole fibre of a point of
s_{-a}
which is contained in
\ell
); hence
\ell
has to be equal to
l_{00}
or
l_{10}
. This yields
(3)
.

We can now assume
a>0
and show
(1)
and
(2)
by proving when
l_{00}
and
l_{10}
are invariant. The surface
\pi^{-1}(s_{-a})\simeq\mathbb{F}_{c}
being invariant, the curve
l_{00}
is invariant when
c<0
and
l_{10}
is invariant when
c>0
. Moreover, the fibres of the
\mathbb{F}_{b}
-bundle
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
(given in Remark
3.1.4
) are exchanged by
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
. If
b>0
, the surface
S_{-b}
given by
x_{0}=0
is the union of the negative sections and is then invariant, so
l_{00}=S_{-b}\cap\pi^{-1}(s_{-a})
is invariant.

It remains to show that
l_{10}
and
l_{00}
are not invariant in the other cases. If
c\leq 0
, the group
\mathbb{G}_{a}
acts on
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
via
(t,[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}])\mapsto([x_{0}:x_{1}+tx_{0}y_{1}^{b}z_{1}^{-c};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}])
,
so
l_{10}
is not invariant. If
b=0
and
c\geq 0
, then
\mathbb{G}_{a}
acts on
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
via
(t,[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}])\mapsto([x_{0}+tx_{1}z_{0}^{c}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}])
,
so
l_{00}
is not invariant.
∎

Lemma 5.4.2
.

(1)

For all
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
with
a,b\geq 0
, the blow-up of the curve
l_{00}\subset\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
given by
x_{0}=y_{0}=0
followed by the contraction of the strict transform of the surface
\pi^{-1}(s_{-a})
onto
l_{10}\subset\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a}
given by
x_{1}=y_{0}=0
yields a birational map

\begin{array}[]{rccc}\varphi\colon&\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}&\dashrightarrow&\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a}\\
&\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\big)&\mapsto&\big([x_{0}:x_{1}y_{0};y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\big).\end{array}

We then have
\varphi\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})\varphi^{-1}\subset\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a})
if and only if
ab>0
or
ac<0
, and we have
\varphi^{-1}\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a})\varphi\subset\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
if and only if
a(c+a)>0
.

(2)

For all
a,b,c\in\mathbb{Z}
with
a,b\geq 0
, every
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
-equivariant birational map of
\,\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow X
is a composition of birational maps as in
(1)

(
and of their inverses
)
and of isomorphisms of
\,\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles.

Proof.

As we can check in local coordinates, the birational map
\mathbb{F}_{b}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{b+1}
given by
[x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}]\mapsto

[x_{0}:x_{1}y_{0};y_{0}:y_{1}]

is the composition of the blow-up of the point
[0:1;0:1]
, followed by the contraction of the strict transform of
y_{0}=0
onto the point
[1:0;0:1]
. Doing this within a family yields
\varphi
. We then have
\varphi\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})\varphi^{-1}\subset\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a})
if and only if
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
preserves
l_{00}
and
\varphi^{-1}\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a})\varphi\subset\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
if and only if
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a})
preserves
l_{10}
. Hence,
(1)
follows from Lemma
5.4.1
for
b\geq 0
.

It remains to prove
(2)
. By Lemma
5.3.1
, we only need to consider elementary links
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow X
, obtained by blowing up an invariant curve
\ell\subset\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
, followed by contracting
\pi^{-1}(\pi(\ell))
. The only curves in
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
that are invariant are
l_{00}
and
l_{10}
(see Lemma
5.4.1
), and the links associated to these are given in
(1)
: if we start with
l_{00}
, then it is equal to
\varphi
as in
(1)
; if we start with
l_{10}
, then it is equal to
\varphi^{-1}
as in
(1)
when
b\geq 1
and is the composition of the isomorphism
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,c}\simeq\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,-c}
exchanging
x_{0}
and
x_{1}
with a link
\varphi
as in
(1)
if
b=0
.
∎

We recall that the notions of stiff and superstiff
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle, used in the next result and later, were defined in the introduction (Definition
1.2.3
).

Corollary 5.4.3
.

Let
a,b\geq 0
and
c\in\mathbb{Z}
be such that
c\leq 0
when
b=0
. Then,
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
is maximal if and only if
a\neq 1
and one of the following holds:

(1)

a=0
; i.e.
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
is a decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{F}_{0}=\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
;

(2)

b=c=0
; i.e.
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,0}\simeq\mathbb{F}_{a}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
;

(3)

-a<c<ab
.

Moreover,
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
is superstiff in cases
(1)
and
(2)
and is not stiff in case
(3)
.

More precisely, denoting by
r
the smallest integer such that
c-ra\leq 0
, we find
r\leq b
and get an infinite sequence of elementary links

\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b-r,c-ra}\dashrightarrow\dots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a}\dashrightarrow\dots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+n,c+an}\dashrightarrow\dots

which conjugates
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
to
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+s,c+as})
for each integer
s\geq-r
. This gives all
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
-equivariant square birational maps from
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
to another
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle.

Proof.

If
a=1
, then
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
is not maximal, by Lemma
5.2.2
. We can thus assume
a\neq 1
. In this case, as we have a surjective group homomorphism
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})\twoheadrightarrow\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{a})
, every
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
-equivariant square birational map starting from
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
is in fact the composition of an element of
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
with an
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
-equivariant birational map of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles. These are compositions of links given in Lemma
5.4.2
(1)
and isomorphisms of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles, as explained in Lemma
5.4.2
(2)
.

If
a=0
or
b=c=0
, then every
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
-equivariant birational map of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles starting from
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
is in fact an isomorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles (see Lemma
5.4.2
). This shows that
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}
is superstiff and thus that
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
is maximal in these cases, corresponding to
(1)
and
(2)
.

We then assume
a\geq 2
and suppose
(b,c)\neq(0,0)
.

Let us show that
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
is not maximal when
c\geq ab
. Note that
b>0
in this case, by assumption (we suppose
c<0
when
b=0
). Lemma
5.4.2
yields an
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
-equivariant birational map
\psi\colon\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,c-ab}
given as the composition of the links
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b-1,c-a}{\dashrightarrow}\dots{\dashrightarrow}\mathcal{F}_{a}^{1,c-a(b-1)}{\dashrightarrow}\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,c-ab}
. By construction,
\psi\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})\psi^{-1}\subset\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,c-ab})
preserves the curve
l_{00}
, which then implies that
\psi\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})\psi^{-1}\subsetneq\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,c-ab})
by Lemma
5.4.1
(1)
.

We now prove that
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
is not maximal when
c\leq-a
. In this case, we use the
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
-equivariant link
\varphi\colon\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a}
given by
Lemma
5.4.2
, which is made such that
\varphi\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})\varphi^{-1}\subset\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a})
preserves the curve
l_{10}
and thus yields
\varphi\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})\varphi^{-1}\subsetneq\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a})
by Lemma
5.4.1
(1)
.

We can now assume
-a<c<ab
. Denoting by
r
smallest integer such that
c-ra\leq 0
, we find
r\leq b
and get an infinite sequence of elementary links

\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b-r,c-ra}\dashrightarrow\cdots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+1,c+a}\dashrightarrow\dots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+n,c+an}\dashrightarrow\dots

which conjugates
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
to
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b+s,c+as})
for each integer
s\geq-r
(see Lemma
5.4.2
). Every
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c})
-equivariant birational map of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow X
is a composition of birational maps such as these and of isomorphisms of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles since
l_{10}
is not invariant by
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b-r,c-ra})
(see Lemma
\ref{Lem:InvariantDec}\ref{Invl1})
as
c-ra\leq 0
and
b-r\geq 0
.
∎

5.5. Links between Umemura bundles over Hirzebruch surfaces

We can similarly treat the case of Umemura
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles. Recall that such bundles
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
are defined by positive integers
a,b\geq 1
and
c\geq 2
such that
c=ak+2
with
0\leq k\leq b
(see Definition
3.6.1
and Remark
3.6.2
for more details).

We start with the case where
a=1
and study the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\mathcal{V}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
obtained from
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b,2}\to\mathbb{F}_{1}
as follows.

Lemma 5.5.1
.

Let
\eta\colon\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
[y_{0}:y_{1};z_{0}:z_{1}]\mapsto[y_{0}z_{0}:y_{1}:y_{0}z_{1}]
be the blow-up of
\,[0:1:0]
, which induces an isomorphism between
\hat{U}=\eta^{-1}(U)
and
U=\mathbb{P}^{2}\setminus\{[0:1:0]\}
.

(1)

For each integer
b\geq 1
, there exist a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\pi\colon\mathcal{V}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
, unique up to isomorphism of
\,\mathbb{P}^{1}
-bundles, and a birational morphism
\psi\colon\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b,2}\to\mathcal{V}_{b}
such that the following hold:

(
i
)

\psi
is a square birational map over
\eta
;

(
ii
)

\psi
induces an isomorphism
\hat{\pi}^{-1}(\hat{U})\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\pi^{-1}(U)
;

(
iii
)

\psi
is the blow-up of the smooth rational curve
\pi^{-1}([0:1:0])\subset\mathcal{V}_{b}
.

(2)

If
L\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
is a line, the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\pi^{-1}(L)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\pi}}{{\to}}L\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}
is isomorphic to

\begin{array}[]{lll}\mathbb{F}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}&\text{ if $L$ is a line through $[0:1:0]$;}\\
\mathbb{F}_{\lvert b-2\rvert}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}&\text{ if $L$ is a line not passing through $[0:1:0]$}.\end{array}

(3)

The image in
\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})
of
\Autz(\mathcal{V}_{b})
is equal to
\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})
if
b=1
and to
\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},[0:1:0])
if
b\geq 2
.

(4)

We have
\psi\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b,2})\psi^{-1}\subset\Autz(\mathcal{V}_{b})
, with equality if
b\geq 2
.

Proof.

The existence of a unique birational map
\psi\colon\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b,2}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{V}_{b}
satisfying
(1)
(1)(i )
-
(1)(ii )
follows from the descent lemma (Lemma
2.3.2
). We now prove that
\psi
also satisfies
(1)(iii )
, which implies that
\psi
is a birational morphism. To do this, we denote by
W\subset\mathbb{F}_{1}
the open subset given by
y_{1}\neq 0
and show that
\hat{\pi}\colon\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b,2}\to\mathbb{F}_{1}
is trivial over
W
. As
W
contains the exceptional curve
s_{-1}\subset\mathbb{F}_{1}
of
\eta
(given by
y_{0}=0
), this will show that one can contract
\hat{\pi}^{-1}(s_{-1})\simeq\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
and obtain a birational morphism having the desired properties. To show the triviality of
\hat{\pi}
over
W
, we take the transition function of
\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b,2}
, given by
\nu\in\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\})
as follows

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\nu\colon\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z\big)&\mapsto&\left([x_{0}:x_{1}z^{2}+x_{0}y_{1}^{b}z;y_{0}z:y_{1}],\frac{1}{z}\right).\end{array}

The intersection of
W
with each chart is isomorphic to
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{2}
, via the inclusion
\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{A}^{2}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
given by
([x_{0}:x_{1}],y,z)\mapsto[x_{0}:x_{1};y:1],z)
, and the transition function becomes

([x_{0}:x_{1}],y,z)\mapsto\linebreak([x_{0}:x_{1}z^{2}+x_{0}z],yz,z^{-1})
, which yields a trivial
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle since
\begin{bmatrix}0&1\\
-1&z^{-1}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}1&0\\
z&z^{2}\end{bmatrix}\cdot\begin{bmatrix}1&-z\\
0&1\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}z&0\\
0&z\end{bmatrix}
. This achieves the proof of
(1)
.

(2)
-
(3)
-
(4)
The existence and
unicity of
\psi
and
\mathcal{V}_{b}
being proven, we then observe that
\psi\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b,2})\psi^{-1}\subset\Autz(\mathcal{V}_{b})
also follows from the descent lemma (see Lemma
2.3.2
). This shows in particular that the subgroup
H_{b}\subset\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})
, being the image of
\Autz(\mathcal{V}_{b})
, contains the group
\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},[0:1:0])\simeq\GL_{2}\rtimes\mathrm{k}^{2}
.

We now take the open subsets
U_{0},U_{1}\subset\mathbb{P}^{2}
given by
U_{0}=\{[X:Y:Z]|X\neq 0\}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}
and
U_{1}=

\{[X:Y:Z]|Z\neq 0\}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{2}
and observe that
\hat{U}_{i}=\eta^{-1}(U_{i})\simeq U_{i}
for
i=0,1
. Hence,
\pi^{-1}(U_{i})\simeq\hat{\pi}^{-1}(\hat{U}_{i})
, and the transition function is computed as follows: a point
[1:u:v]\in U_{0}\cap U_{1}
corresponds to the point
[1:u;1:v]\in\mathbb{F}_{1}
, and thus its preimage to
([x_{0}:x_{1};1:u],v)\in\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
on the first chart is sent onto
([x_{0}:x_{1}v^{2}+x_{0}u^{b}v;v:u],v^{-1})=([x_{0}:x_{1}v^{2-b}+x_{0}u^{b}v^{1-b};1:v^{-1}],v^{-1})
on the second chart. The transition function is then given by

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathbb{P}^{1}\times U_{0}&\dashrightarrow&\mathbb{P}^{1}\times U_{1}\\
\big([x_{0}:x_{1}],[1:u:v]\big)&\mapsto&\big([x_{0}:x_{1}v^{2-b}+x_{0}u^{b}v^{1-b}],[\frac{1}{v}:\frac{u}{v}:1]\big).\end{array}

For
b=1
, we find the transition function of
\mathcal{S}_{1}\simeq\mathbb{P}(T_{\mathbb{P}^{2}})
(see Corollary
4.2.2
), which yields
\mathcal{V}_{b}\simeq\mathcal{S}_{1}
and thus
H_{1}=\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})
(see Remark
4.2.3
or Lemma
4.2.5
(2)
). In particular,
\psi\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b,2})\psi^{-1}\subsetneq\Autz(\mathcal{V}_{b})
in this case. Assertions
(3)
and
(4)
are then proven for
b=1
.

We now prove
(2)
(for each
b\geq 1
). We observe that if
L
passes through
[0:1:0]
, its strict transform on
\mathbb{F}_{1}
is a fibre
f
of the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
, so
\hat{\pi}^{-1}(f)\simeq\mathbb{F}_{b}
. Since
\pi^{-1}(L)\simeq\hat{\pi}^{-1}(f)
(because
\psi
is a blow-up of a curve), we obtain
\pi^{-1}(L)\simeq\mathbb{F}_{b}
. We now take a line
L
not passing through
[0:1:0]
, use the action of
\Autz(\mathcal{V}_{b})
to restrict to the case where
L
is the line given by
Y=0
, and set
u=0
in the transition function above to get
\pi^{-1}(L)\simeq\mathbb{F}_{\lvert b-2\rvert}
.

Assertion
(3)
for
b\geq 2
is now given as follows:
[0:1:0]
has to be fixed by
H_{b}
because of
(2)
, and
\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},[0:1:0])\subset H_{b}
was already proven.

It remains to show
(4)
, and then to prove that every element
g\in\Autz(\mathcal{V}_{b})
belongs to
\psi^{-1}g\psi\in\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b,2})
when
b\geq 2
. Assertion
(3)
implies that
g
preserves the curve
\pi^{-1}([0:1:0])
, so this follows from
(1)
(1)(iii )
.∎

Lemma 5.5.2
.

Let
a,b\geq 1
and
c\geq 2
be such that
c=ak+2
with
0\leq k\leq b
. The curves of
\,\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}
invariant by
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})
are given as follows:

(1)

The curve
l_{00}
given by
x_{0}=y_{0}=0
on both charts is invariant.

(2)

The curve
l_{10}
given by
x_{1}=y_{0}=0
on both charts is invariant if and only if
k>0

(
i.e. when
c>2)
.

(3)

These are the two curves
(
respectively, the only curve
)
of
\,\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}
invariant by
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})
if
c>2

(
respectively, when
c=2)
.

Proof.

Since
a\geq 1
, the curve
s_{-a}\subset\mathbb{F}_{a}
is invariant by
\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{a})
; hence the surface
\pi^{-1}(s_{-a})
is given by
y_{0}=0
on both charts. The fibres of the
\mathbb{F}_{b}
-bundle
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{P}^{1}
are exchanged by
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})
. Since
b\geq 1
, the surface
S_{-b}
given by
x_{0}=0
is the union of the negative sections and is then invariant. This yields
(1)
.

Recall that the transition function of
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}
is given by

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\nu\colon\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z\big)&\mapsto&\left([x_{0}:x_{1}z^{c}+x_{0}y_{0}^{k}y_{1}^{b-k}z^{c-1};y_{0}z^{a}:y_{1}],\frac{1}{z}\right)\end{array}

(see Remark
3.6.2
).
If
k>0
, the surface
\pi^{-1}(s_{-a})
, corresponding to
y_{0}=0
, is isomorphic to
\mathbb{F}_{c}
, and the curve
l_{10}
given by
x_{1}=y_{0}=0
on both charts corresponds to the curve
s_{-c}\subset\mathbb{F}_{c}
(with
c>0
) and is thus invariant.

It remains to see that
l_{10}
is not invariant if
k=0
and that no curve distinct from
l_{10}
or
l_{00}
can be invariant.

(3)
Let
\ell\subset\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}
be an invariant curve. As the morphism
\pi\colon\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
yields a surjective group homomorphism
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})\twoheadrightarrow\Autz(\mathbb{F}_{a})
(see Lemma
3.6.3
), and because
a\geq 1
, we have
\pi(\ell)=s_{-a}
. We then use the fact that
\ell
has to be invariant by the
\GL_{2}
-action given explicitly in Remark
3.6.5
. We consider the action of the upper-triangular group by taking
\gamma=0
and obtain that the image of
([x_{0}:x_{1};0:1],0)
, on the first chart, is equal to
\big([x_{0}:\frac{x_{1}\alpha^{c-1}}{\delta};0:1],0\big)
if
k>0
and to
\big([x_{0}:\frac{x_{1}\alpha^{c-1}}{\delta}-\frac{x_{0}\alpha^{c-2}\beta}{\delta};0:1],0\big)
if
k=0
. We then find that either
([0:1;0:1],0)\in\ell
or
([1:0;0:1],0)\in\ell
and
k>0
. In the first case, we get
\ell=l_{00}
since
l_{00}
is an orbit. In the second case,
l_{10}
is an orbit and
\ell=l_{10}
. This achieves the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.5.3
.

(1)

For each Umemura
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}\to\mathbb{F}_{a}
, the blow-up of the curve
l_{00}\subset\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}
followed by the contraction of the strict transform of the surface
\pi^{-1}(s_{-a})
onto the curve
\ell_{10}
yields a birational map

\varphi\colon\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+1,c+a}
satisfying
\varphi\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})\varphi^{-1}=\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+1,c+a})
.

(2)

For each
a\geq 1
, we have a birational map
\varphi\colon\mathcal{U}_{a}^{1,a+2}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,2}
such that
\varphi(\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{1,a+2}))\varphi^{-1}\subsetneq\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,2})
.

Proof.

(1)

For each
a\geq 1,b\geq 0
, and
0\leq k\leq b
, we write
c=ak+2
, denote by
\nu_{a}^{b,c}\in\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\})
the birational map

\begin{array}[]{rccc}&\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}&\dashrightarrow&\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\\
\nu_{a}^{b,c}\colon&\big([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z\big)&\mapsto&\left([x_{0}:x_{1}z^{c}+x_{0}y_{0}^{k}y_{1}^{b-k}z^{c-1};y_{0}z^{a}:y_{1}],\frac{1}{z}\right),\end{array}

and observe that
\nu_{a}^{b,c}
is the transition function of
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}
if
b\geq 1
(see Remark
3.6.2
). We then denote by
\varphi_{b}
the birational map

\begin{array}[]{rccc}&\mathbb{F}_{b}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}&\dashrightarrow&\mathbb{F}_{b+1}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}\\
\varphi_{b}\colon&([x_{0}:x_{1};y_{0}:y_{1}],z)&\mapsto&([x_{0}:x_{1}y_{0};y_{0}:y_{1}],z)\end{array}

and observe that
\varphi_{b}\nu_{a}^{b,c}\varphi_{b}^{-1}=\nu_{a}^{b+1,c+a}
. If
b\geq 1
, the blow-up of
l_{00}\subset\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}
, followed by the contraction of the strict transform of the surface
\pi^{-1}(s_{-a})
, yields a birational map given in the two charts by
\varphi_{b}
. This then corresponds to a birational map
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+1,c+a}
, which is the blow-up of the curve
l_{00}\subset\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}
followed by the contraction of the strict transform of the surface
\pi^{-1}(s_{-a})
. Since
l_{00}
and
\pi^{-1}(s_{-a})
are invariant by
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})
(see Lemma
5.5.2
), we get
\varphi\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})\varphi^{-1}\subset\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+1,c+a})
. We then observe that
\varphi^{-1}
is the blow-up of
l_{10}\subset\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+1,c+a}
followed by the contraction of the strict transform of the surface
\pi^{-1}(s_{-a})
. As
l_{10}
is invariant by
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+1,c+a})
, we obtain
\varphi\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})\varphi^{-1}=\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+1,c+a})
. This achieves the proof of
(1)
.

We now consider the above construction in the case
b=0
(which yields
k=0
and
c=2
). The transition function
\nu_{a}^{b+1,c+a}
still corresponds to the transition function of
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+1,c+a}=\mathcal{U}_{a}^{1,a+2}
, but the transition function
\nu_{a}^{b,c}
corresponds to a transition function on a
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle over
\mathbb{F}_{a}
with numerical invariants
(a,b,c)
, which is therefore decomposable and isomorphic to
\mathcal{F}_{a}^{b,c}=\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,2}
(see Proposition
3.3.1
(2)
). The maps
(\varphi_{b})^{-1}
on both charts then yield an elementary link
\varphi\colon\mathcal{U}_{a}^{1,a+2}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,2}
centred at
l_{10}
, which is then
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{1,a+2})
-equivariant. We moreover have
\varphi(\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{1,a+2}))\varphi^{-1}\subsetneq\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,2})
since
\Autz(\mathcal{F}_{a}^{0,2})
contains a torus of dimension
3
(see Remark
3.1.3
), which is not the case for
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{1,a+2})
(as follows from Remark
3.6.4
). This then achieves the proof of
(2)
.
∎

Corollary 5.5.4
.

Let
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}
be an Umemura bundle. Then,
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})
is maximal if and only if one of the following holds:

(1)

a\geq 2
and
c-ab<2
;

(2)

a=1
and
c-ab<1
.

In this case,
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}
is not stiff. More precisely, denoting by
k
the integer such that
c=ak+2
, we find
0\leq k<b
and get a sequence of birational maps

\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b-k,2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{-k}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b-k+1,2+a}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{-1}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{0}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{n-1}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+n,c+na}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{n}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}\cdots

such that
\varphi_{n}\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+n,2+na})\varphi_{n}^{-1}=\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+n+1,2+(n+1)a})
for each
n\geq-k
. If
a\geq 2
, this gives all
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})
-equivariant square birational maps from
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}
to another
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle. If
a=1
, we add the birational morphism
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b-k,2}=\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b-k,2}\to\mathcal{V}_{b-k,1}
of Lemma
5.5.1
.

Proof.

We denote as usual by
k
the integer satisfying
0\leq k\leq b
such that
c=ak+2
and find that
c-ab<2\Leftrightarrow k<b
.

If
c-ab\geq 2
, then
k=b
, so
c=ab+2
. We construct an
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})
-equivariant birational map of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{a}^{1,a+2}
which is the composition of birational maps
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}=\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,ab+2}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b-1,a(b-1)+2}\dashrightarrow\dots\dashrightarrow\mathcal{U}_{a}^{1,a+2}
(see Lemma
5.5.3
(1)
). Since
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{1,a+2})
is not maximal (see Lemma
5.5.3
(2)
), neither is
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})
.

We now assume
c-ab<2
, which means
k<b
. Lemma
5.5.3
(1)
yields a sequence of birational maps

\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b-k,2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{-k}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b-k+1,2+a}\dots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{-1}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{0}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}\cdots\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{n-1}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+n,c+na}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varphi_{n}}}{{\dashrightarrow}}\cdots

such that
\varphi_{n}\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+n,2+na})\varphi_{n}^{-1}=\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b+n+1,2+(n+1)a})
for each
n\geq-k
.

If
a\geq 2
, every
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})
-equivariant birational map of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c}\dashrightarrow X
is a composition of birational maps as these and of isomorphisms of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles since
l_{10}
is not invariant by
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b-k,2})
(see Lemma
\ref{Lem:InvariantUme})
. We then get the result in this case (
a\geq 2
).

It remains to do the case where
a=1
, which yields
c=k+2
and
c-ab=k-b+2
. If
c-ab=1
, then
b-k=1
, which implies that
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b-k,2})
is not maximal (see Lemma
5.5.1
) and thus neither is
\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b,c})
. If
c-ab<1
, then
b-k>2
. We thus get a birational morphism
\psi\colon\mathcal{U}_{a}^{b-k,2}\to\mathcal{V}_{b-k}
which satisfies
\psi\Autz(\mathcal{U}_{1}^{b-k,2})\psi^{-1}=\Autz(\mathcal{V}_{b-k})
(see Lemma
5.5.1
). It remains to show that we cannot get any further link. At the level of surfaces, the only
\Aut(\mathbb{F}_{1})
-equivariant birational maps
\mathbb{F}_{1}\dashrightarrow S
, where
S
is a smooth projective surface, are isomorphisms or blow-ups
\mathbb{F}_{1}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
of a point of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. We then only need to show that there is no square birational map
\mathcal{V}_{b-k}\dashrightarrow X
, to a
\pi
-bundle
X\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
, which is not a square isomorphism. We can reduce to the case of birational of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles (doing nothing on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
) and then use Lemma
5.3.1
, and only need to observe that no curve of
\mathbb{P}^{2}
is invariant by the action of
\Autz(\mathcal{V}_{b-k})
, which acts as
\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},[0:1:0])
. The result then follows from Lemma
5.3.1
.
∎

5.6. Links between Schwarzenberger bundles

Lemma 5.6.1
.

Let
b\geq 1
, and let
\pi\colon\mathcal{S}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the
b
-th Schwarzenberger
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle. If
\,b=1
, no curve of
\mathcal{S}_{b}
is invariant by
\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})
. If
b\geq 2
, there is a unique curve invariant by
\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})
, which is given on the two charts of Lemma
4.2.1
by

\begin{array}[]{l}\left\{\left([x_{0}:x_{1}],([1:2t:t^{2}])\right)\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\times U_{0}\mid x_{0}+tx_{1}=0\right\},\\[4.30554pt]
\left\{\left([x_{0}:x_{1}],([t^{2}:2t:1])\right)\in\mathbb{P}^{1}\times U_{1}\mid x_{0}-tx_{1}=0\right\}.\end{array}

Proof.

Let
\rho\colon\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})\to\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})
be the group homomorphism induced by
\pi
.
If
b=1
, then
\rho
is surjective (see Lemma
4.2.5
(2)
), so there is no curve of
\mathcal{S}_{b}
which is invariant. Now suppose
b\geq 2
, in which case
\rho
yields an isomorphism
\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})\xrightarrow{\vbox to0.0pt{\vss\hbox{$\scriptstyle\simeq$}\vskip-2.0pt}}\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2},\Gamma)\simeq\PGL_{2}
(again by Lemma
4.2.5
(2)
). Every curve of
\mathcal{S}_{b}
is then contained in the invariant surface
X=\pi^{-1}(\Gamma)\subset\mathcal{S}_{b}
. To understand the action of
\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})\simeq\PGL_{2}
on
X
, we use the corresponding action on the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\hat{\pi}\colon\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{b}=\mathcal{S}_{b}\times_{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1})\to\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
, obtained by Lemma
4.2.4
. The pull-back of
X
on
\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{b}
is the surface
\hat{X}=\hat{\pi}(\Delta)\subset\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{b}
(where
\Delta\subset\mathbb{P}^{1}\times\mathbb{P}^{1}
is the diagonal), isomorphic to
X
via a
\PGL_{2}
-equivariant isomorphism. By Lemma
4.2.4
, there is a unique curve in
\hat{X}
invariant by
\Autz(\hat{\mathcal{S}}_{b})\simeq\PGL_{2}
, which is sent onto the curve of
X
given locally as above.
∎

Lemma 5.6.2
.

Let
b\geq 2
, and let
\pi\colon\mathcal{S}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be the
b
-th Schwarzenberger
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle. There is a birational involution
\varphi\colon\mathcal{S}_{b}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{b}
such that
\varphi\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})\varphi^{-1}=\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})
. Moreover, every
\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})
-equivariant birational map of
\,\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle
\mathcal{S}_{b}\dashrightarrow X
is either an isomorphism of
\,\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles or a composition of
\varphi
with an isomorphism of
\,\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.6.1
, there is a unique curve
D\subset\mathcal{S}_{b}
which is invariant by
\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})
and satisfies
\pi(D)=\Gamma
. We consider the birational involutions

\begin{array}[]{rcccccc}\varphi_{0}\colon&\mathbb{P}^{1}\times U_{0}&\dashrightarrow&\mathbb{P}^{1}\times U_{0}\\
&\big([x_{0}:x_{1}],[1:u:v]\big)&\mapsto&\big([-ux_{0}-2vx_{1}:2x_{0}+ux_{1}],[1:u:v]\big),\\[4.30554pt]
\varphi_{1}\colon&\mathbb{P}^{1}\times U_{1}&\dashrightarrow&\mathbb{P}^{1}\times U_{1}\\
&\big([x_{0}:x_{1}],[v:u:1]\big)&\mapsto&\big([ux_{0}-2vx_{1}:2x_{0}-vx_{1}],[v:u:1]\big)\end{array}

which correspond locally to the blow-up of
D
, followed by the contraction of the strict transform of
\pi^{-1}(\Gamma)
, in the two charts (see Lemma
5.6.1
for the equation of
D
). We then check that
\varphi_{1}\theta=\theta\varphi_{0}
, where
\theta\colon\mathbb{P}^{1}\times U_{0}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{P}^{1}\times U_{1}
is the transition function of
\mathcal{S}_{b}
given in Lemma
4.2.1
. This follows from the equality

\begin{array}[]{ll}\begin{bmatrix}-s-t&2\\
-2st&s+t\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}s^{b}-t^{b}&st(s^{b-1}-t^{b-1})\\
s^{b+1}-t^{b+1}&st(s^{b}-t^{b})\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}s^{b}+t^{b}&st(s^{b-1}+t^{b-1})\\
s^{b+1}+t^{b+1}&st(s^{b}+t^{b})\end{bmatrix}\\[8.61108pt]
=\begin{bmatrix}s^{b}-t^{b}&st(s^{b-1}-t^{b-1})\\
s^{b+1}-t^{b+1}&st(s^{b}-t^{b})\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}s+t&2st\\
-2&-s-t\end{bmatrix}\end{array}

and then yields a birational map of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\varphi\colon\mathcal{S}_{b}\dashrightarrow\mathcal{S}_{b}
, given by the blow-up of
D
, followed by the contraction of the strict transform of
\pi^{-1}(\Gamma)
onto
D
.

By Lemma
5.3.1
, every birational map of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
\mathcal{S}_{b}\to X
which is not an isomorphism is a composition of
\varphi
with an isomorphism of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles.
∎

Corollary 5.6.3
.

Let
b\geq 1
. Then,
\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})
is maximal and
\mathcal{S}_{b}
is stiff. It is moreover superstiff if and only if
b=1
.

Proof.

If
b=1
, the
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle has no invariant curves (see Remark
4.2.3
) (it is actually a homogeneous variety), and we conclude by Lemma
5.3.1
. If
b\geq 2
, we apply Lemma
5.6.2
.
∎

5.7. Rigidity for decomposable bundles over
\mathbb{P}^{2}

Lemma 5.7.1
.

Let
b\geq 0
, and let
\pi\colon\mathcal{P}_{b}\to\mathbb{P}^{2}
be a decomposable
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle.

(1)

\mathcal{P}_{b}
does not contain any
\Autz(\mathcal{S}_{b})
-invariant curve.

(2)

\Autz(\mathcal{P}_{b})
is maximal and
\mathcal{P}_{b}
is superstiff.

Proof.

Assertion
(1)
follows from the fact that
\Autz(\mathcal{P}_{b})
surjects onto
\Aut(\mathbb{P}^{2})
(see Lemma
4.1.2
).

(2)
Let
\varphi\colon\mathcal{P}_{b}\dashrightarrow X
be an
\Autz(\mathcal{P}_{b})
-square birational map of
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles, over
\eta\colon\mathbb{P}^{2}\dashrightarrow X
(where
X
is a smooth projective rational surface). We want to show that
\varphi
is a square isomorphism. The action of
\Autz(\mathcal{P}_{b})
on
\mathbb{P}^{2}
being transitive, the birational map
\eta
is an isomorphism, so we can assume
X=\mathbb{P}^{2}
, and that
\eta
is the identity. We then apply Lemma
5.3.1
to get that
\varphi
is an isomorphism.
∎

5.8. Last step

We have all the ingredients needed to prove the main theorems of this paper stated in the introduction.

Proof of Theorems
A
and
B
.

These are simply a consequence of Proposition
5.1.1
, together with Corollaries
5.4.3
,
5.5.4
,
5.6.3
and Lemma
5.7.1
.
∎

Remark 5.8.1
.

Our original motivation was to study the maximal connected algebraic subgroups of the Cremona group
\Bir(\mathbb{P}^{3})
. These are studied in the article
[
BFT21
]
. Most of the families appearing in our classification in fact give maximal connected algebraic subgroups of the Cremona group, even if some sporadic cases (like
\mathcal{S}_{2}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
) disappear as they are conjugate to bigger subgroups, with a birational map which does not preserve any
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundle structure.

Remark 5.8.2
.

If we assume the characteristic of the algebraically closed field
\mathrm{k}
to be positive, the strategy to prove Theorems
A
and
B
should be analogous, but some of the results that we use in characteristic zero are no longer valid in positive characteristic; see
e.g.
Remarks
3.7.3
and
3.7.5
. As a consequence, it seems that new
\mathbb{P}^{1}\!
-bundles
X\to S
analogous to Umemura bundles and Schwarzenberger bundles, and such that
\Autz(X)
is maximal, could show up in the classification. The positive characteristic case will be studied by the authors in a future work.
